1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{forest_dj}
7	\caption{Judge severity estimates ($d_j$) for all nine judges with 94\% credible intervals. Positive values indicate stricter scoring. Proprietary judges (Claude Opus, GPT-5.5) cluster at the strict end; open-source judges (Mistral, LLaMA) are more lenient. The severity range spans 1.34 logits.}
8	\label{fig:judge_severity}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	\subsection{Setup}
12	\label{sec:setup}
13	
14	\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 316 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. After filtering for coverage, the evaluation set comprises 176 items in a connected incomplete design (XX{,}XXX observations; XX.X\% fill rate of the full $N \times I \times J$ tensor). Seventeen target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated: the original 8 models (GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, Qwen2.5-72B, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen3-8B, Gemma-2-9B, GPT-4-0613, GPT-3.5-Turbo) plus 9 additional models (Claude-3.5-Sonnet, GPT-4-Turbo, LLaMA-3.1-70B, Gemma-2-27B, Claude-3-Opus, LLaMA-3-70B, Mixtral-8x22B, Claude-3-Haiku, LLaMA-3-8B). Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \citep{zheng2023mtbench}.
15	
16	\paragraph{Judges.} Nine LLM judges score model responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.7, Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Mistral-Large-2, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Seven judges cover all 17 target models; two judges (GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7) cover only the original 8 models, yielding an asymmetric connected design---Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only connectivity, not full crossing. Position swapping controls for position bias and doubles the effective observation count. Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs systematic self-evaluation bias, and excluding self-judging observations leaves rankings unchanged ($\tau = 0.857$; Appendix~\ref{app:self_eval}).
17	
18	\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated via a two-source pipeline (\S\ref{sec:staged}): reference model anchoring with gpt-4-0314 as anchor (75\% weight) and cross-model agreement (25\% weight). The pipeline is robust to weight configuration (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.998$ across all configurations tested).
19	
20	\paragraph{MCMC.} We use NumPyro's NUTS sampler with 4{,}000 warmup and 4{,}000 sampling iterations across 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). The contamination prior follows the hierarchical HalfNormal specification (Eq.~\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\hat{R}$ \citep{vehtari2021rhat} with conventional $\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS $> 100$ thresholds \citep{gelman2013bda}.
21	
22	\subsection{Baselines and Main Results}
23	\label{sec:main_results}
24	
25	We compare G-MFRM against seven methods that address judge bias or contamination separately: (1) Raw Average, (2) MFRM-only (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}), (3) Sequential pipeline (MFRM then contamination adjustment), (4) CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026}, (5) CyclicJudge+discard (exposed items removed), (6) DCR Factor \citep{dcr2025}, and (7) Preference Leakage calibration \citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\tau$ and Spearman $\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.
26	
27	\begin{table}[t]
28	\centering
29	\small
30	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
31	\toprule

40	CyclicJudge+discard & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\

47	\caption{Correlation with Chatbot Arena human rankings on Arena-Hard (9 judges). All seven baselines produce identical rankings at $N = 8$ ($\tau = 0.786$). G-MFRM yields nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($+$7.1pp; bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ CI includes zero). Right columns report the 17-model evaluation (TBD).}
48	\label{tab:main_results}
49	\end{table}
50	
51	\begin{figure*}[t]
52	\centering
53	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig6_cn_profile}
54	\caption{Per-model contamination diagnostics from G-MFRM. (a)~Posterior estimates of contamination susceptibility $\hat{c}_n$ with 95\% credible intervals, ordered by magnitude. Older models (GPT-3.5-Turbo, GPT-4-0613) show 4--6$\times$ higher susceptibility than recent models (GPT-4o). (b)~Ability bias absorbed by MFRM-only estimation: $\Delta\theta = \hat{\theta}_{\mathrm{MFRM}} - \hat{\theta}_{\mathrm{G\text{-}MFRM}}$. The perfect rank correlation (Spearman $\rho = 1.0$) confirms that contamination inflates MFRM ability estimates for high-$c_n$ models and deflates them for low-$c_n$ models, as predicted by the OVB analysis (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}).}
55	\label{fig:cn_profile}
56	\end{figure*}
57	
58	All seven baselines produce identical model rankings ($\tau = 0.786$, $\rho = 0.905$). With $N = 8$, Kendall $\tau$ takes only ${\sim}20$ distinct values, but raw ability scores also vary by $< 0.03$ logits across methods (Table~\ref{tab:raw_scores}), confirming genuine invariance rather than discretization artifact. This reflects the structural confounding from Section~\ref{sec:confounding}: single-facet corrections leave the uncorrected confound intact (Observation~\ref{obs:ovb}). That CyclicJudge+discard also preserves rankings suggests contamination effects on aggregate rankings are modest at this scale; G-MFRM's primary value lies in per-model contamination diagnostics. G-MFRM achieves nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$, $\rho = 0.952$) over the baselines ($\tau = 0.786$), but the posterior median $\tau$ equals the baseline (0.786), with the point estimate at the distribution's upper tail. Bootstrap resampling (4{,}000 posterior draws) yields $\Delta\tau$ 95\% confidence interval (CI) $[-0.071, 0.071]$: the ranking improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$, though G-MFRM almost never degrades rankings ($P(\Delta\tau \geq 0) = 0.927$). On synthetic data ($N = 50$), joint estimation significantly outperforms sequential ($\tau = 0.922$ vs.\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming that the effect is real but below detection at this sample size. The rank change---gpt-4o-mini and qwen2.5-72b swap positions---is consistent with differential contamination ($P(\theta_{\text{mini}} > \theta_{\text{qwen}}) = 0.853$).
59	
60	We evaluate G-MFRM along two orthogonal axes: predictive fit and ranking accuracy.
61	
62	\paragraph{Predictive fit.} Leave-one-out (LOO) cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM (expected log pointwise predictive density $\Delta\mathrm{ELPD}_{\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, standard error [SE] $= 9.4$, $|\Delta/\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable LOO estimates. The contamination gating term substantially improves held-out predictive fit.
63	
64	\paragraph{Ranking accuracy.} Ranking improvement ($\Delta\tau = +0.071$) is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\% CI crosses zero). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \approx 17$ is needed for 80\% power to detect this effect size (Appendix~\ref{app:power}). This prediction is empirically validated: at $N = 20$, synthetic $\Delta\tau$ reaches significance (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$, 18 wins / 1 loss; Appendix~\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}). Pooling 20 replications at $N = 8$ also yields significance ($p < 0.002$), confirming the method consistently improves rankings though any single $N = 8$ dataset has limited power.
65	
66	The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\hat{R} \leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 and minimum tail ESS of 381 (model-level parameters: bulk ESS $\geq$ 217, tail ESS $\geq$ 437). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to posterior overconfidence (synthetic CI coverage 71\%; see Limitations); we report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit mean-square residuals [MNSQ] within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix~\ref{app:item_diagnostics}). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age, directionally consistent with known training data exposure (Figure~\ref{fig:cn_profile}a). Estimated $\hat{c}_n$ exceeds 1.0 for the four oldest models: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($\hat{c}_n = 5.60$), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$). The most recent models show low estimated contamination: GPT-4o ($0.88$) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$). The global shrinkage scale $\hat{\sigma}_c = 2.40$ confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\bar{c} \approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\hat{\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}). A per-model diagnostic (Figure~\ref{fig:cn_profile}b; Table~\ref{tab:bias_absorption}) reveals that MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (GPT-3.5-Turbo), while deflating estimates for the least contaminated models by up to 0.40 logits (GPT-4o). Judge severity $d_j$ separates cleanly by provenance: proprietary judges score more strictly than open-source judges, with the full severity range spanning 1.34 logits (Figure~\ref{fig:judge_severity}; Table~\ref{tab:judge_severity}). Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe \citep{piironen2017horseshoe} vs.\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix~\ref{app:prior_robustness}) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \geq 2$, with $\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\ref{app:judge_count}).
67	
68	\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}
69	\label{sec:error_prop_results}
70	
71	Section~\ref{sec:error_prop} predicted that ability estimates degrade gracefully when exposure noise $\sigma$ remains below $\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)} \approx 0.2$. We validate this prediction by injecting Gaussian noise into the true exposure values and re-fitting G-MFRM on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds; exp-012).
72	
73	Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} confirms this prediction: below $\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\%; beyond the threshold ($\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial. Rank ordering is preserved even at $\sigma = 0.5$ ($\tau > 0.88$).
74	
75	Ablation (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}, Appendix) confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor: staged estimation increases $\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\times$ ($p < 0.001$); continuous gating improves $c_n$-RMSE by 40\% over binary ($p < 0.001$). Removing $d_j$ preserves rankings but degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6\%, $p < 0.001$).
76

All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\\tau$ and Spearman $\\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{8 models}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{17 models}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\tau$ & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nRaw Average & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\\\\nMFRM-only & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\\\\nSequential & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\\\\nCyclicJudge & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\\\\nCyclicJudge+discard & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\\\\nDCR Factor & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\\\\nPref.\\ Leakage & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\\\\n\\midrule\nG-MFRM (ours) & \\textbf{0.857} & \\textbf{0.952} & \\textbf{TBD} & \\textbf{TBD} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Correlation with Chatbot Arena human rankings on Arena-Hard (9 judges). All seven baselines produce identical rankings at $N = 8$ ($\\tau = 0.786$). G-MFRM yields nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($+$7.1pp; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero). Right columns report the 17-model evaluation (TBD).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig6_cn_profile}\n\\caption{Per-model contamination diagnostics from G-MFRM. (a)~Posterior estimates of contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ with 95\\% credible intervals, ordered by magnitude. Older models (GPT-3.5-Turbo, GPT-4-0613) show 4--6$\\times$ higher susceptibility than recent models (GPT-4o). (b)~Ability bias absorbed by MFRM-only estimation: $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{G\\text{-}MFRM}}$. The perfect rank correlation (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$) confirms that contamination inflates MFRM ability estimates for high-$c_n$ models and deflates them for low-$c_n$ models, as predicted by the OVB analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).}\n\\label{fig:cn_profile}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nAll seven baselines produce identical model rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$, $\\rho = 0.905$). With $N = 8$, Kendall $\\tau$ takes only ${\\sim}20$ distinct values, but raw ability scores also vary by $< 0.03$ logits across methods (Table~\\ref{tab:raw_scores}), confirming genuine invariance rather than discretization artifact. This reflects the structural confounding from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}: single-facet corrections leave the uncorrected confound intact (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). That CyclicJudge+discard also preserves rankings suggests contamination effects on aggregate rankings are modest at this scale; G-MFRM's primary value lies in per-model contamination diagnostics. G-MFRM achieves nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$, $\\rho = 0.952$) over the baselines ($\\tau = 0.786$), but the posterior median $\\tau$ equals the baseline (0.786), with the point estimate at the distribution's upper tail. Bootstrap resampling (4{,}000 posterior draws) yields $\\Delta\\tau$ 95\\% confidence interval (CI) $[-0.071, 0.071]$: the ranking improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$, though G-MFRM almost never degrades rankings ($P(\\Delta\\tau \\geq 0) = 0.927$). On synthetic data ($N = 50$), joint estimation significantly outperforms sequential ($\\tau = 0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming that the effect is real but below detection at this sample size. The rank change---gpt-4o-mini and qwen2.5-72b swap positions---is consistent with differential contamination ($P(\\theta_{\\text{mini}} > \\theta_{\\text{qwen}}) = 0.853$).\n\nWe evaluate G-MFRM along two orthogonal axes: predictive fit and ranking accuracy.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictive fit.} Leave-one-out (LOO) cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM (expected log pointwise predictive density $\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, standard error [SE] $= 9.4$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable LOO estimates. The contamination gating term substantially improves held-out predictive fit.\n\n\\paragraph{Ranking accuracy.} Ranking improvement ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$) is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI crosses zero). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \\approx 17$ is needed for 80\\% power to detect this effect size (Appendix~\\ref{app:power}). This prediction is empirically validated: at $N = 20$, synthetic $\\Delta\\tau$ reaches significance (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$, 18 wins / 1 loss; Appendix~\\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}). Pooling 20 replications at $N = 8$ also yields significance ($p < 0.002$), confirming the method consistently improves rankings though any single $N = 8$ dataset has limited power.\n\nThe MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 and minimum tail ESS of 381 (model-level parameters: bulk ESS $\\geq$ 217, tail ESS $\\geq$ 437). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \\citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \\citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to posterior overconfidence (synthetic CI coverage 71\\%; see Limitations); we report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit mean-square residuals [MNSQ] within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix~\\ref{app:item_diagnostics}). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age, directionally consistent with known training data exposure (Figure~\\ref{fig:cn_profile}a). Estimated $\\hat{c}_n$ exceeds 1.0 for the four oldest models: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($\\hat{c}_n = 5.60$), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$). The most recent models show low estimated contamination: GPT-4o ($0.88$) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$). The global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}). A per-model diagnostic (Figure~\\ref{fig:cn_profile}b; Table~\\ref{tab:bias_absorption}) reveals that MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (GPT-3.5-Turbo), while deflating estimates for the least contaminated models by up to 0.40 logits (GPT-4o). Judge severity $d_j$ separates cleanly by provenance: proprietary judges score more strictly than open-source judges, with the full severity range spanning 1.34 logits (Figure~\\ref{fig:judge_severity}; Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}). Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe \\citep{piironen2017horseshoe} vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix~\\ref{app:prior_robustness}) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}\n\\label{sec:error_prop_results}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:error_prop} predicted that ability estimates degrade gracefully when exposure noise $\\sigma$ remains below $\\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)} \\approx 0.2$. We validate this prediction by injecting Gaussian noise into the true exposure values and re-fitting G-MFRM on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds; exp-012).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} confirms this prediction: below $\\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial. Rank ordering is preserved even at $\\sigma = 0.5$ ($\\tau > 0.88$).\n\nAblation (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}, Appendix) confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor: staged estimation increases $\\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\\times$ ($p < 0.001$); continuous gating improves $c_n$-RMSE by 40\\% over binary ($p < 0.001$). Removing $d_j$ preserves rankings but degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6\\%, $p < 0.001$).\n",

```text
1	\section{The G-MFRM Framework}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_error_propagation}
7	\caption{Error propagation from Stage~2 exposure noise to Stage~3 parameter estimates. Ability RMSE ($\theta$, left) and contamination RMSE ($c_n$, right) as a function of exposure noise $\sigma_e$. The dashed line marks $\sigma_{\mathrm{crit}} \approx 0.2$, below which degradation remains mild ($\theta$-RMSE $+$5.9\%, $c_n$-RMSE $+$7.9\%). Error bars: $\pm$1 std over 20 seeds.}
8	\label{fig:error_prop}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	Section~\ref{sec:confounding} established that omitting contamination from the measurement model produces directional bias in ability estimates (Observation~\ref{obs:ovb}). We now introduce the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly estimates ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single measurement framework. The key design choices, staged estimation with externally fixed exposure and a hierarchical HalfNormal prior for the contamination term, are motivated by the identifiability analysis in \S\ref{sec:identifiability} and the error propagation bounds in \S\ref{sec:error_prop}.
12	
13	\subsection{Model Specification}
14	\label{sec:model_spec}
15	
16	G-MFRM extends the standard MFRM \citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} by adding a multiplicative contamination gating term to the linear predictor (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}):
17	%
18	\begin{equation}
19	\label{eq:gmfrm}
20	\log \frac{P(Y_{nij} \geq k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k)} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i,
21	\end{equation}
22	%
23	where all terms carry the definitions from Section~\ref{sec:confounding}. The gating structure $c_n \cdot e_i$ separates the model-specific and item-specific components of contamination: $c_n \geq 0$ captures how much model $n$ benefits from memorized benchmark content, while $e_i \in [0,1]$ quantifies how much of item $i$ has leaked into training corpora. When $c_n = 0$ (a clean model) or $e_i = 0$ (a novel item), the gating term vanishes and Eq.~\ref{eq:gmfrm} reduces to the standard MFRM. This graceful degradation ensures that G-MFRM imposes no distortion on uncontaminated model-item pairs.
24	
25	We enforce non-negativity and sparsity of $c_n$ through a hierarchical HalfNormal prior. A global shrinkage scale $\sigma_c \sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ controls the overall contamination level, and per-model raw effects $c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n \sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ are scaled to obtain $c_n = c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n \cdot \sigma_c$. The HalfNormal support restricts $c_n \geq 0$, and the hierarchical structure provides adaptive shrinkage: when the data support low overall contamination, $\sigma_c$ contracts toward zero, pulling all $c_n$ values down. Models with genuine contamination retain large $c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n$ relative to the group. The remaining priors are $\theta_n \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $b_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $d_j \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$, and standard uniform on thresholds $\tau_k$.
26	
27	The closest antecedent is the Doubly-Gated IRT model of \citet{shu2013dgirt}, which introduced binary gating for item preknowledge in educational testing. Beyond extending DG-IRT to continuous exposure and multi-rater designs, G-MFRM's primary methodological contributions are analytical: the identifiability analysis (Propositions~\ref{prop:no_new}--\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}) provides the first formal conditions under which gated IRT parameters are separable, and the Murphy--Topel error propagation framework (\S\ref{sec:error_prop}) quantifies how staged estimation uncertainty propagates to downstream parameter estimates---neither of which exists for DG-IRT or any prior gated measurement model.
28	
29	\subsection{Staged Estimation}
30	\label{sec:staged}
31	
32	Joint estimation of all parameters in Eq.~\ref{eq:gmfrm}, including $e_i$, would require identifying the bilinear product $c_n \cdot e_i$ from response data alone. This creates a fundamental non-identifiability (Proposition~\ref{prop:exposure_variation}). We resolve this through a three-stage procedure that fixes exposure externally before estimating the contamination parameters.
33	
34	\paragraph{Stage 1: Standard MFRM.} We fit the standard MFRM (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}) via MCMC (NUTS) to obtain initial estimates $(\hat{\theta}, \hat{b}, \hat{d})$. These serve as warm-start values for the sampler in Stage~3 and improve convergence.
35	
36	\paragraph{Stage 2: Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated from external evidence independent of the response data used in Stages~1 and~3. The primary signal is \emph{reference model anchoring}: using a fixed reference model (e.g., gpt-4-0314) as anchor, we compute win-rate residuals for each target model against difficulty-adjusted expectations; items where target models systematically outperform the anchor beyond what difficulty predicts are flagged as potentially exposed. The secondary signal is \emph{cross-model agreement}: items receiving uniformly high scores across architecturally diverse models suggest exposure rather than genuine easiness, quantified via $z$-score standardization of the inter-model agreement distribution. These signals are combined as a weighted composite (75\% anchoring, 25\% agreement), clipped to $[0, 1]$, and treated as fixed in Stage~3. Where model log-probabilities are accessible, LogProber can provide an additional gray-box memorization signal, though it is not used in our current experiments.
37	
38	\paragraph{Non-circularity.} Exposure estimation is not circular: $e_i$ is item-level (content leakage) while $c_n$ is model-level (susceptibility), testing a cross-level interaction. The primary signal (75\%) derives from reference anchoring independent of target models, and ablation with 100\% anchoring yields $\rho = 0.998$ (Appendix~\ref{app:weight_robustness}), confirming $e_i$ is robust to removing the cross-model component.
39	
40	\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed, we sample $(\theta_n, b_i, d_j, c_n)$ jointly via the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) \citep{betancourt2015}. The priors are as specified in \S\ref{sec:model_spec}. The hierarchical contamination prior decomposes as
41	%
42	\begin{align}
43	\label{eq:contam_prior}
44	\sigma_c &\sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \quad c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n \sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \nonumber \\
45	c_n &= c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n \cdot \sigma_c,
46	\end{align}
47	%
48	where $\sigma_c$ is a shared global scale that governs the overall contamination magnitude. This non-centered parameterization improves sampler geometry by decoupling the global scale from the per-model effects.
49	
50	Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\hat{R}$ \citep{vehtari2021rhat} and bulk effective sample size (ESS), with thresholds $\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS$_{\mathrm{bulk}} > 100$ following standard practice \citep{gelman2013bda}.\footnote{Synthetic validation with $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$ yields satisfactory convergence across all parameters (exp-012). Full diagnostic tables are reported in Section~\ref{sec:experiments}.}
51	
52	
53	\subsection{Identifiability}
54	\label{sec:identifiability}
55	
56	\begin{figure}[t]
57	\centering
58	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_cn_recovery}
59	\caption{Contamination susceptibility recovery ($c_n$: true vs.\ estimated) from 50 Monte Carlo seeds. Empirical CI coverage is 71\% (below nominal 95\%), indicating moderate posterior overconfidence (see Limitations).}
60	\label{fig:cn_recovery}
61	\end{figure}
62	
63	Adding a multiplicative term to a measurement model raises the question of whether the new parameters can be separated from the existing ones. We establish that fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for identification. All proofs assume the standard location constraint $\sum_n \theta_n = 0$.
64	
65	\begin{proposition}[No New Indeterminacy]
66	\label{prop:no_new}
67	When $e_i$ is fixed externally, the gating term $c_n \cdot e_i$ introduces no additional location or scale indeterminacy beyond that already present in the standard MFRM.
68	\end{proposition}
69	
70	\begin{proof}
71	Suppose $c_n' = c_n + \delta$ preserves all response probabilities. Then $\delta \cdot e_i = 0$ for all items. Since $e_i \neq 0$ for at least one item by assumption, $\delta = 0$. The key observation is that fixing $e_i$ externally eliminates the bilinear ambiguity $c_n \cdot e_i = (\lambda c_n)(\lambda^{-1} e_i)$ that would otherwise render the decomposition non-unique.
72	\end{proof}
73	
74	\begin{proposition}[Exposure Variation Necessary]
75	\label{prop:exposure_variation}
76	If $e_i = e$ for all $i$ (constant exposure), then $c_n$ and $\theta_n$ are not separately identifiable.
77	\end{proposition}
78	
79	\begin{proof}
80	When exposure is constant, the linear predictor becomes $(\theta_n + c_n \cdot e) - b_i - d_j - \tau_k$, which is equivalent to a standard MFRM with reparameterized ability $\theta^*_n = \theta_n + c_n \cdot e$. No configuration of response data can distinguish $(\theta_n, c_n)$ from $(\theta_n + \epsilon, c_n - \epsilon/e)$ for any $\epsilon$.
81	\end{proof}
82	
83	\begin{corollary}
84	$\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ is a necessary condition for identifiability of $c_n$.
85	\end{corollary}
86	
87	\begin{theorem}[Sufficient Conditions for Local Identifiability]
88	\label{thm:sufficient}
89	The G-MFRM is locally identifiable, up to the standard location constraint, if the following conditions hold: (i) a connected observation design (each item is observed by at least one model--judge pair), (ii) $J \geq 2$ judges, (iii) for each model $n$, the set of observed items spans at least two distinct exposure values ($\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ over observed items), and (iv) standard normalization $\sum_n \theta_n = 0$.
90	\end{theorem}
91	
92	The proof, given in Appendix~\ref{app:proof}, follows \citet{rothenberg1971}: non-singularity of the Fisher information matrix is established via the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality on the $(\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix determinant and a Schur complement argument for the remaining blocks. Conditions~(i) and~(ii) are standard multi-rater design requirements, satisfied by the connected design in our evaluation (XX.X\% fill rate; \S\ref{sec:setup}); condition~(iii) is the substantive constraint that each model's observed items include varying exposure levels.
93	
94	\subsection{Error Propagation}
95	\label{sec:error_prop}
96	
97	The staged procedure treats the Stage~2 exposure estimates $\hat{e}_i$ as fixed quantities in Stage~3. This is a generated regressors problem \citep{pagan1984}: measurement error in $\hat{e}_i$ propagates into the posterior of $(\theta_n, c_n)$. The multiplicative structure $c_n \cdot e_i$ makes this an errors-in-variables (EIV) problem \citep{fuller1987}, with a predictable attenuation pattern.
98	
99	Let $\hat{e}_i = e_i + \epsilon_i$ with $\epsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)$ independent of all model parameters. The attenuation factor for the contamination coefficient is
100	%
101	\begin{equation}
102	\label{eq:attenuation}
103	\lambda(\sigma) = \frac{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}{\mathrm{Var}(e_i) + \sigma^2},
104	\end{equation}
105	%
106	so attenuation reaches 50\% when $\sigma = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$. In the synthetic data-generating process ($\mathrm{Var}(e_i) \approx 0.04$), this corresponds to $\sigma \approx 0.2$.\footnote{The attenuation formula assumes that $\epsilon_i$ is independent of all model parameters, identically distributed, and Gaussian. In practice, exposure estimation errors may be correlated across items sharing the same source or heteroscedastic across difficulty levels. Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} provides empirical validation that $\hat{\theta}$ estimates remain robust under such departures (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).}
107	
108	Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} shows the empirical degradation across noise levels (Figure~\ref{fig:error_prop}). Below the critical threshold ($\sigma \leq 0.2$), degradation is mild: $\theta$-RMSE increases by 5.9\% (from $0.128 \pm 0.018$ to $0.135 \pm 0.018$) and $c_n$-RMSE by 7.9\% (from $0.533 \pm 0.187$ to $0.575 \pm 0.198$). At $\sigma = 0.1$, the increases are only 1.4\% and 2.3\% respectively. Beyond the threshold ($\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes severe: $\theta$-RMSE rises 36.9\% and $c_n$-RMSE rises 57.9\%. Contamination estimates $c_n$ are more sensitive than ability estimates $\theta_n$ because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$ (multiplicative coupling), while $\theta_n$ contributes additively and is anchored by all observations regardless of exposure.
109	
110	\begin{table}[t]
111	\centering
112	\small
113	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
114	\toprule
115	$\sigma$ & $\theta$-RMSE & $\Delta\theta$\% & $c_n$-RMSE & $\Delta c_n$\% \\
116	\midrule
117	0.0 & 0.128 $\pm$ 0.018 & --- & 0.533 $\pm$ 0.187 & --- \\
118	0.1 & 0.130 $\pm$ 0.018 & +1.4 & 0.545 $\pm$ 0.192 & +2.3 \\
119	0.2 & 0.135 $\pm$ 0.018 & +5.9 & 0.575 $\pm$ 0.198 & +7.9 \\
120	0.5 & 0.175 $\pm$ 0.027 & +36.9 & 0.842 $\pm$ 0.281 & +57.9 \\
121	\bottomrule
122	\end{tabular}
123	\caption{Error propagation robustness (exp-012). Ability RMSE degrades by less than 6\% when exposure noise $\sigma \leq 0.2$, matching the theoretical prediction. Contamination estimates are more sensitive due to multiplicative coupling with $e_i$.}
124	\label{tab:error_prop}
125	\end{table}
126	
127	These bounds make the staged approximation falsifiable: if the noise level of the Stage~2 exposure estimates can be bounded below $\sigma_{\mathrm{crit}}$, the resulting ability estimates carry quantifiable error guarantees. For honest credible intervals that account for generated-regressor uncertainty, we apply the Murphy-Topel variance correction \citep{murphy1985} to inflate the Stage~3 posterior variance by the estimated Stage~2 sampling variability (Appendix~\ref{app:murphy_topel}).
128	
129	\subsection{Fair Scores and Diagnostics}
130	\label{sec:fair_scores}
131	
132	The Stage~3 posterior mean of $\theta_n$ provides a bias-and-contamination-corrected ``fair score'' for each model. The remaining parameters serve complementary diagnostic roles: per-model $c_n$ flags candidates for training data audit, per-judge $d_j$ informs judge calibration, and per-item $e_i$ identifies items for retirement or replacement.
```

Ability RMSE ($\\theta$, left) and contamination RMSE ($c_n$, right) as a function of exposure noise $\\sigma_e$. The dashed line marks $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}} \\approx 0.2$, below which degradation remains mild ($\\theta$-RMSE $+$5.9\\%, $c_n$-RMSE $+$7.9\\%). Error bars: $\\pm$1 std over 20 seeds.}\n\\label{fig:error_prop}\n\\end{figure}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:confounding} established that omitting contamination from the measurement model produces directional bias in ability estimates (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). We now introduce the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly estimates ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single measurement framework. The key design choices, staged estimation with externally fixed exposure and a hierarchical HalfNormal prior for the contamination term, are motivated by the identifiability analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:identifiability} and the error propagation bounds in \\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}.\n\n\\subsection{Model Specification}\n\\label{sec:model_spec}\n\nG-MFRM extends the standard MFRM \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} by adding a multiplicative contamination gating term to the linear predictor (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}):\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:gmfrm}\n\\log \\frac{P(Y_{nij} \\geq k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k)} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere all terms carry the definitions from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}. The gating structure $c_n \\cdot e_i$ separates the model-specific and item-specific components of contamination: $c_n \\geq 0$ captures how much model $n$ benefits from memorized benchmark content, while $e_i \\in [0,1]$ quantifies how much of item $i$ has leaked into training corpora. When $c_n = 0$ (a clean model) or $e_i = 0$ (a novel item), the gating term vanishes and Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm} reduces to the standard MFRM. This graceful degradation ensures that G-MFRM imposes no distortion on uncontaminated model-item pairs.\n\nWe enforce non-negativity and sparsity of $c_n$ through a hierarchical HalfNormal prior. A global shrinkage scale $\\sigma_c \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ controls the overall contamination level, and per-model raw effects $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ are scaled to obtain $c_n = c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c$. The HalfNormal support restricts $c_n \\geq 0$, and the hierarchical structure provides adaptive shrinkage: when the data support low overall contamination, $\\sigma_c$ contracts toward zero, pulling all $c_n$ values down. Models with genuine contamination retain large $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n$ relative to the group. The remaining priors are $\\theta_n \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $b_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $d_j \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$, and standard uniform on thresholds $\\tau_k$.\n\nThe closest antecedent is the Doubly-Gated IRT model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt}, which introduced binary gating for item preknowledge in educational testing. Beyond extending DG-IRT to continuous exposure and multi-rater designs, G-MFRM's primary methodological contributions are analytical: the identifiability analysis (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) provides the first formal conditions under which gated IRT parameters are separable, and the Murphy--Topel error propagation framework (\\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}) quantifies how staged estimation uncertainty propagates to downstream parameter estimates---neither of which exists for DG-IRT or any prior gated measurement model.\n\n\\subsection{Staged Estimation}\n\\label{sec:staged}\n\nJoint estimation of all parameters in Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm}, including $e_i$, would require identifying the bilinear product $c_n \\cdot e_i$ from response data alone. This creates a fundamental non-identifiability (Proposition~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}). We resolve this through a three-stage procedure that fixes exposure externally before estimating the contamination parameters.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 1: Standard MFRM.} We fit the standard MFRM (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}) via MCMC (NUTS) to obtain initial estimates $(\\hat{\\theta}, \\hat{b}, \\hat{d})$. These serve as warm-start values for the sampler in Stage~3 and improve convergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 2: Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated from external evidence independent of the response data used in Stages~1 and~3. The primary signal is \\emph{reference model anchoring}: using a fixed reference model (e.g., gpt-4-0314) as anchor, we compute win-rate residuals for each target model against difficulty-adjusted expectations; items where target models systematically outperform the anchor beyond what difficulty predicts are flagged as potentially exposed. The secondary signal is \\emph{cross-model agreement}: items receiving uniformly high scores across architecturally diverse models suggest exposure rather than genuine easiness, quantified via $z$-score standardization of the inter-model agreement distribution. These signals are combined as a weighted composite (75\\% anchoring, 25\\% agreement), clipped to $[0, 1]$, and treated as fixed in Stage~3. Where model log-probabilities are accessible, LogProber can provide an additional gray-box memorization signal, though it is not used in our current experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-circularity.} Exposure estimation is not circular: $e_i$ is item-level (content leakage) while $c_n$ is model-level (susceptibility), testing a cross-level interaction. The primary signal (75\\%) derives from reference anchoring independent of target models, and ablation with 100\\% anchoring yields $\\rho = 0.998$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:weight_robustness}), confirming $e_i$ is robust to removing the cross-model component.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed, we sample $(\\theta_n, b_i, d_j, c_n)$ jointly via the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) \\citep{betancourt2015}. The priors are as specified in \\S\\ref{sec:model_spec}. The hierarchical contamination prior decomposes as\n%\n\\begin{align}\n\\label{eq:contam_prior}\n\\sigma_c &\\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\quad c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\nonumber \\\\\nc_n &= c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c,\n\\end{align}\n%\nwhere $\\sigma_c$ is a shared global scale that governs the overall contamination magnitude. This non-centered parameterization improves sampler geometry by decoupling the global scale from the per-model effects.\n\nConvergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat} and bulk effective sample size (ESS), with thresholds $\\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS$_{\\mathrm{bulk}} > 100$ following standard practice \\citep{gelman2013bda}.\\footnote{Synthetic validation with $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$ yields satisfactory convergence across all parameters (exp-012). Full diagnostic tables are reported in Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}.}\n\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_cn_recovery}\n\\caption{Contamination susceptibility recovery ($c_n$: true vs.\\ estimated) from 50 Monte Carlo seeds. Empirical CI coverage is 71\\% (below nominal 95\\%), indicating moderate posterior overconfidence (see Limitations).}\n\\label{fig:cn_recovery}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAdding a multiplicative term to a measurement model raises the question of whether the new parameters can be separated from the existing ones. We establish that fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for identification. All proofs assume the standard location constraint $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[No New Indeterminacy]\n\\label{prop:no_new}\nWhen $e_i$ is fixed externally, the gating term $c_n \\cdot e_i$ introduces no additional location or scale indeterminacy beyond that already present in the standard MFRM.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nSuppose $c_n' = c_n + \\delta$ preserves all response probabilities. Then $\\delta \\cdot e_i = 0$ for all items. Since $e_i \\neq 0$ for at least one item by assumption, $\\delta = 0$. The key observation is that fixing $e_i$ externally eliminates the bilinear ambiguity $c_n \\cdot e_i = (\\lambda c_n)(\\lambda^{-1} e_i)$ that would otherwise render the decomposition non-unique.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Exposure Variation Necessary]\n\\label{prop:exposure_variation}\nIf $e_i = e$ for all $i$ (constant exposure), then $c_n$ and $\\theta_n$ are not separately identifiable.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nWhen exposure is constant, the linear predictor becomes $(\\theta_n + c_n \\cdot e) - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k$, which is equivalent to a standard MFRM with reparameterized ability $\\theta^*_n = \\theta_n + c_n \\cdot e$. No configuration of response data can distinguish $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ from $(\\theta_n + \\epsilon, c_n - \\epsilon/e)$ for any $\\epsilon$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\begin{corollary}\n$\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ is a necessary condition for identifiability of $c_n$.\n\\end{corollary}\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Sufficient Conditions for Local Identifiability]\n\\label{thm:sufficient}\nThe G-MFRM is locally identifiable, up to the standard location constraint, if the following conditions hold: (i) a connected observation design (each item is observed by at least one model--judge pair), (ii) $J \\geq 2$ judges, (iii) for each model $n$, the set of observed items spans at least two distinct exposure values ($\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ over observed items), and (iv) standard normalization $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe proof, given in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}, follows \\citet{rothenberg1971}: non-singularity of the Fisher information matrix is established via the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality on the $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix determinant and a Schur complement argument for the remaining blocks. Conditions~(i) and~(ii) are standard multi-rater design requirements, satisfied by the connected design in our evaluation (XX.X\\% fill rate; \\S\\ref{sec:setup}); condition~(iii) is the substantive constraint that each model's observed items include varying exposure levels.\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation}\n\\label{sec:error_prop}\n\nThe staged procedure treats the Stage~2 exposure estimates $\\hat{e}_i$ as fixed quantities in Stage~3. This is a generated regressors problem \\citep{pagan1984}: measurement error in $\\hat{e}_i$ propagates into the posterior of $(\\theta_n, c_n)$. The multiplicative structure $c_n \\cdot e_i$ makes this an errors-in-variables (EIV) problem \\citep{fuller1987}, with a predictable attenuation pattern.\n\nLet $\\hat{e}_i = e_i + \\epsilon_i$ with $\\epsilon_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, \\sigma^2)$ independent of all model parameters. The attenuation factor for the contamination coefficient is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:attenuation}\n\\lambda(\\sigma) = \\frac{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) + \\sigma^2},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nso attenuation reaches 50\\% when $\\sigma = \\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$. In the synthetic data-generating process ($\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) \\approx 0.04$), this corresponds to $\\sigma \\approx 0.2$.\\footnote{The attenuation formula assumes that $\\epsilon_i$ is independent of all model parameters, identically distributed, and Gaussian. In practice, exposure estimation errors may be correlated across items sharing the same source or heteroscedastic across difficulty levels. Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop} provides empirical validation that $\\hat{\\theta}$ estimates remain robust under such departures (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} shows the empirical degradation across noise levels (Figure~\\ref{fig:error_prop}). Below the critical threshold ($\\sigma \\leq 0.2$), degradation is mild: $\\theta$-RMSE increases by 5.9\\% (from $0.128 \\pm 0.018$ to $0.135 \\pm 0.018$) and $c_n$-RMSE by 7.9\\% (from $0.533 \\pm 0.187$ to $0.575 \\pm 0.198$). At $\\sigma = 0.1$, the increases are only 1.4\\% and 2.3\\% respectively. Beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes severe: $\\theta$-RMSE rises 36.9\\% and $c_n$-RMSE rises 57.9\\%. Contamination estimates $c_n$ are more sensitive than ability estimates $\\theta_n$ because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$ (multiplicative coupling), while $\\theta_n$ contributes additively and is anchored by all observations regardless of exposure.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\sigma$ & $\\theta$-RMSE & $\\Delta\\theta$\\% & $c_n$-RMSE & $\\Delta c_n$\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.0 & 0.128 $\\pm$ 0.018 & --- & 0.533 $\\pm$ 0.187 & --- \\\\\n0.1 & 0.130 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +1.4 & 0.545 $\\pm$ 0.192 & +2.3 \\\\\n0.2 & 0.135 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +5.9 & 0.575 $\\pm$ 0.198 & +7.9 \\\\\n0.5 & 0.175 $\\pm$ 0.027 & +36.9 & 0.842 $\\pm$ 0.281 & +57.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Error propagation robustness (exp-012). Ability RMSE degrades by less than 6\\% when exposure noise $\\sigma \\leq 0.2$, matching the theoretical prediction. Contamination estimates are more sensitive due to multiplicative coupling with $e_i$.}\n\\label{tab:error_prop}\n\\end{table}\n\nThese bounds make the staged approximation falsifiable: if the noise level of the Stage~2 exposure estimates can be bounded below $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}}$, the resulting ability estimates carry quantifiable error guarantees. For honest credible intervals that account for generated-regressor uncertainty, we apply the Murphy-Topel variance correction \\citep{murphy1985} to inflate the Stage~3 posterior variance by the estimated Stage~2 sampling variability (Appendix~\\ref{app:murphy_topel}).\n\n\\subsection{Fair Scores and Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:fair_scores}\n\nThe Stage~3 posterior mean of $\\theta_n$ provides a bias-and-contamination-corrected ``fair score'' for each model. The remaining parameters serve complementary diagnostic roles: per-model $c_n$ flags candidates for training data audit, per-judge $d_j$ informs judge calibration, and per-item $e_i$ identifies items for retirement or replacement.",

    }

```text
1	\section{Confounding in LLM Evaluation}
2	\label{sec:confounding}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig2_ovb_bias}
7	\caption{Sequential estimation bias ($\hat{\theta}_{\mathrm{seq}} - \theta_{\mathrm{true}}$) versus true contamination susceptibility $c_n$. Pearson $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$) confirms systematic overestimation of contaminated models. Synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds).}
8	\label{fig:ovb_bias}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	The Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) extends classical Rasch measurement to settings with multiple rater facets \citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm}. Originating in educational measurement, the model treats rater variation as a nuisance facet to be estimated and removed rather than a source of noise to be averaged over. In the LLM evaluation context, where $J$ judges score $N$ models across $I$ items, the MFRM specifies
12	%
13	\begin{equation}
14	\label{eq:mfrm}
15	\log \frac{P(Y_{nij} \geq k \mid \theta_n, b_i, d_j, \tau_k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k \mid \theta_n, b_i, d_j, \tau_k)} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k,
16	\end{equation}
17	%
18	where $Y_{nij}$ is the ordinal rating by judge $j$ for model $n$ on item $i$, $\theta_n$ is the ability of model $n$ ($n = 1, \ldots, N$), $b_i$ is the difficulty of item $i$ ($i = 1, \ldots, I$), $d_j$ is the severity of judge $j$ ($j = 1, \ldots, J$), and $\tau_k$ is the threshold for category $k$. All facets reside on a common logit scale, so model ability is directly comparable after adjustment for item difficulty and judge severity. \citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} applied this framework to compare ten LLM raters with human experts, showing that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects in multi-judge panels.
19	
20	However, standard MFRM assumes that all systematic sources of score variation are captured by the modeled facets. This assumption breaks down when some models have been exposed to benchmark items during training \citep{dcr2025}. Such contamination inflates scores on exposed items through memorization rather than genuine ability. We represent this effect as an interaction $c_n \cdot e_i$, where $c_n \geq 0$ is the contamination susceptibility of model $n$ and $e_i \in [0, 1]$ is the exposure level of item $i$. The complete data-generating process becomes
21	%
22	\begin{equation}
23	\label{eq:full}
24	\eta_{nijk} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i.
25	\end{equation}
26	
27	\begin{figure}[t]
28	\centering
29	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_bias_vs_rho}
30	\caption{Sequential estimator bias remains approximately constant across confounding levels ($\rho$), confirming omission-driven rather than confounding-strength-dependent bias. Joint G-MFRM estimation reduces bias by approximately 70\% at all $\rho$ levels. Synthetic data: $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 50 seeds.}
31	\label{fig:bias_vs_rho}
32	\end{figure}
33	
34	When a practitioner fits the standard MFRM (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}) to data generated by Eq.~\ref{eq:full}, the contamination term is absent from the estimated model. Its influence does not vanish but is absorbed into the estimated parameters $\tilde{\theta}_n$, $\tilde{b}_i$, and $\tilde{d}_j$, which distorts all downstream inferences.
35	
36	The consequence of this omission can be stated precisely.
37	
38	\begin{observation}[Omitted Variable Bias in MFRM]
39	\label{obs:ovb}
40	Let the full model be $\eta_{nijk} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i$ and the short model omit the contamination term: $\tilde{\eta}_{nijk} = \tilde{\theta}_n - \tilde{b}_i - \tilde{d}_j - \tau_k$. Under a linear approximation to the ordinal logit link \citep{wooldridge2010, angrist2009mostly},\footnote{The OVB formula is exact for linear models. We apply it to the MFRM linear predictor $\eta$, which is an approximation. Synthetic validation confirms the approximation quality: the Pearson correlation between the predicted bias direction and empirical bias reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), indicating that the linear result accurately characterizes bias direction and relative magnitude.}
41	\begin{equation}
42	\label{eq:ovb}
43	\mathrm{plim}(\tilde{\theta}_n - \theta_n) = \gamma_n \cdot c_n,
44	\end{equation}
45	where $\gamma_n$ depends on the projection of the exposure vector $(e_i)$ onto the design matrix of the short model (explicit form in Appendix~\ref{app:gamma}). The bias is non-zero whenever $\mathrm{Cov}(d_j,\, c_n \cdot e_i) \neq 0$. This captures the confounding component; in the nonlinear MFRM, a specification bias from omitting $c_n \cdot e_i$ persists even at zero covariance (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
46	\end{observation}
47	
48	The linear approximation in Observation~\ref{obs:ovb} ($r = 0.86$) serves solely as directional motivation for the bias structure; G-MFRM's Stage~3 inference (Eq.~\ref{eq:gmfrm}) uses the full nonlinear ordinal logistic likelihood and does not depend on this approximation. The bias combines a specification component from omitting $c_n \cdot e_i$, which persists regardless of confounding, with a confounding component from non-orthogonality between $d_j$ and $c_n \cdot e_i$. Monte Carlo simulation confirms that bias magnitude is approximately constant across confounding levels $\rho \in \{0, 0.3, 0.6, 0.9\}$, indicating that the omission of $c_n \cdot e_i$---not its correlation with $d_j$---is the primary driver (Figure~\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho}).
49	
50	This confounding undermines both sequential estimation strategies. A bias-first approach, as in CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026}, calibrates $d_j$ before estimating ability, but the resulting $\hat{\theta}$ is already inflated for contaminated models because the omitted $c_n \cdot e_i$ term has been absorbed into the ability estimates. A contamination-first approach, as in DCR \citep{dcr2025}, adjusts for contamination before addressing judge effects, but judge severity is then misattributed as contamination signal. Synthetic validation confirms this failure: with $N = 50$ models, $I = 100$ items, $J = 8$ judges, and 20 random seeds, the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$). As illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:ovb_bias}, the bias is not random but directional: contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings.
```

Under a linear approximation to the ordinal logit link \\citep{wooldridge2010, angrist2009mostly},\\footnote{The OVB formula is exact for linear models. We apply it to the MFRM linear predictor $\\eta$, which is an approximation. Synthetic validation confirms the approximation quality: the Pearson correlation between the predicted bias direction and empirical bias reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), indicating that the linear result accurately characterizes bias direction and relative magnitude.}\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:ovb}\n\\mathrm{plim}(\\tilde{\\theta}_n - \\theta_n) = \\gamma_n \\cdot c_n,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\gamma_n$ depends on the projection of the exposure vector $(e_i)$ onto the design matrix of the short model (explicit form in Appendix~\\ref{app:gamma}). The bias is non-zero whenever $\\mathrm{Cov}(d_j,\\, c_n \\cdot e_i) \\neq 0$. This captures the confounding component; in the nonlinear MFRM, a specification bias from omitting $c_n \\cdot e_i$ persists even at zero covariance (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{observation}\n\nThe linear approximation in Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb} ($r = 0.86$) serves solely as directional motivation for the bias structure; G-MFRM's Stage~3 inference (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm}) uses the full nonlinear ordinal logistic likelihood and does not depend on this approximation. The bias combines a specification component from omitting $c_n \\cdot e_i$, which persists regardless of confounding, with a confounding component from non-orthogonality between $d_j$ and $c_n \\cdot e_i$. Monte Carlo simulation confirms that bias magnitude is approximately constant across confounding levels $\\rho \\in \\{0, 0.3, 0.6, 0.9\\}$, indicating that the omission of $c_n \\cdot e_i$---not its correlation with $d_j$---is the primary driver (Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho}).\n\nThis confounding undermines both sequential estimation strategies. A bias-first approach, as in CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, calibrates $d_j$ before estimating ability, but the resulting $\\hat{\\theta}$ is already inflated for contaminated models because the omitted $c_n \\cdot e_i$ term has been absorbed into the ability estimates. A contamination-first approach, as in DCR \\citep{dcr2025}, adjusts for contamination before addressing judge effects, but judge severity is then misattributed as contamination signal. Synthetic validation confirms this failure: with $N = 50$ models, $I = 100$ items, $J = 8$ judges, and 20 random seeds, the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$). As illustrated in Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}, the bias is not random but directional: contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings.",

      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 50
    }

1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}
5	LLM-as-a-judge evaluations are subject to multiple systematic biases. \citet{wang2024positionbias} demonstrate that LLM judges exhibit strong position bias in pairwise evaluation, where swapping the order of candidate responses can flip the ranking; \citet{li2024splitmerge} propose a split-and-merge strategy to align position biases across evaluators. Beyond positional effects, \citet{koo2024cobbler} benchmark six cognitive biases in 15 LLM evaluators, finding significant bias in approximately 40\% of comparisons, and \citet{ye2024justice} quantify verbosity and self-enhancement biases. \citet{panickssery2024selffavor} show that LLMs systematically favor their own generations, a confound that affects any evaluation where a judge model shares a family with a target. On the correction side, CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias via round-robin assignment but treats residual variation as genuine ability. Unsupervised Debiasing Alignment (UDA) \citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\% without modeling contamination. \citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty} formalize judge variability via $J/\Delta J$ diagnostics. \citet{bavaresco2025judgebench} evaluate 11 LLMs as judges across 20 NLP tasks, finding large variance across datasets and emphasizing the need for task-specific validation. All of these methods model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination effects into ability estimates (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Benchmark Contamination Detection and Avoidance.}
8	Benchmark contamination arises when evaluation items appear in training data, inflating performance estimates. Detection methods take multiple approaches: ConStat \citep{constat2024} uses performance-based statistical tests comparing original and rephrased items; Data Contamination Risk (DCR) \citep{dcr2025} quantifies contamination via a unified metric integrating multiple evidence sources; LogProber \citep{logprober2024} leverages token-level log-probabilities to disentangle memorization from genuine capability; and Min-K\% \citep{mink2024} detects pretraining data membership through outlier token probabilities. \citet{golchin2024timetravel} propose guided instruction prompting to detect instance-level contamination, while \citet{oren2024proving} provide provable statistical guarantees for black-box contamination detection. \citet{deng2024contamination} and \citet{sainz2023contamination} investigate the prevalence and measurement of contamination across modern benchmarks. On the judge side, \citet{preference_leakage2026} identify preference leakage, where judge-side familiarity with benchmark items inflates scores---a complementary phenomenon to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus. Contamination avoidance strategies include dynamic benchmarks such as LiveBench \citep{livebench2025}, private evaluation via TRUCE \citep{truce2024}, and efficient subset selection via tinyBenchmarks \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks}. G-MFRM takes a complementary approach: rather than preventing contamination, it corrects for residual contamination in existing benchmarks while simultaneously modeling judge effects.
9	
10	\paragraph{Item Response Theory (IRT) and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}
11	Item Response Theory provides a principled measurement framework for jointly modeling item difficulty and examinee ability. \citet{lalor2019irt} demonstrate that IRT item parameters can be learned from model ensembles (``artificial crowds'') without human responses. \citet{rodriguez2021irt} apply IRT to NLP leaderboards, showing that IRT-based rankings can identify annotation errors and highlight informative evaluation items. \citet{vania2021irt} use IRT to compare 29 NLP datasets across 18 models, revealing which benchmarks are saturated versus discriminative. In the multi-rater setting, the Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) \citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} extends IRT to separate rater effects from examinee ability. \citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply MFRM to compare ten LLM raters with human experts, demonstrating that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects. \citet{uto2020generalized} propose a generalized MFRM with Bayesian estimation; \citet{choi2026irt_judge} diagnose LLM judge reliability via IRT. Pseudo-Siamese Network for IRT (PSN-IRT) \citep{zhou2025psnirt} extends IRT with neural architectures but does not include contamination facets. The Doubly-Gated IRT (DG-IRT) model of \citet{shu2013dgirt} is the closest antecedent, introducing binary gating for item preknowledge in educational testing; G-MFRM extends this to continuous exposure, multi-rater designs, and provides formal identifiability analysis not available for DG-IRT (Section~\ref{sec:model_spec}).
12	
```

```text
1	\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}}
2	\label{app:proof}
3	
4	We prove that the G-MFRM is locally identifiable under the conditions of Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}. Following \citet{rothenberg1971}, a parametric model is locally identifiable at the true parameter vector $\boldsymbol{\psi}_0$ if and only if the Fisher information matrix $\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_0)$ is non-singular.
5	
6	\paragraph{Setup.} The G-MFRM parameter vector is $\boldsymbol{\psi} = (\boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{c}, \mathbf{b}, \mathbf{d}, \boldsymbol{\tau})$, where $\boldsymbol{\theta} \in \mathbb{R}^{N-1}$ (after the location constraint $\sum_n \theta_n = 0$), $\mathbf{c} \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^N$, $\mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^I$, $\mathbf{d} \in \mathbb{R}^J$, and $\boldsymbol{\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^{K-1}$. The exposure vector $\mathbf{e} = (e_1, \ldots, e_I)$ is fixed externally and treated as known. The linear predictor is $\eta_{nijk} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i$, with partial derivatives:
7	%
8	\begin{align*}
9	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial \theta_n} &= 1, \quad
10	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial c_n} = e_i, \quad
11	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial b_i} = -1, \\
12	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial d_j} &= -1, \quad
13	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial \tau_k} = -1.
14	\end{align*}
15	
16	\paragraph{Fisher information weights.} For each observation cell $(n,i,j)$, the Fisher information weight under the cumulative logit model is
17	%
18	\begin{equation}
19	\label{eq:fisher_weight}
20	w_{nij} = \sum_{k=1}^{K} \frac{\bigl[\sigma'(\eta_{nijk}) - \sigma'(\eta_{nij,k+1})\bigr]^2}{P(Y_{nij} = k)},
21	\end{equation}
22	%
23	where $\sigma(x) = (1 + e^{-x})^{-1}$, $\sigma'(x) = \sigma(x)(1 - \sigma(x))$, and we set $\sigma'(\eta_{nij,0}) = \sigma'(\eta_{nij,K+1}) = 0$ at the boundaries. Since $P(Y_{nij} = k) > 0$ for all categories and $\sigma'(\cdot) > 0$, we have $w_{nij} > 0$ for all $(n,i,j)$.
24	
25	\paragraph{Step 1: The $(\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix.} For each model $n$, the $2 \times 2$ Fisher information submatrix is
26	%
27	\begin{equation}
28	\label{eq:fisher_block}
29	\mathbf{F}_n = \sum_{i=1}^{I} \sum_{j=1}^{J} w_{nij}
30	\begin{pmatrix} 1 & e_i \\ e_i & e_i^2 \end{pmatrix}.
31	\end{equation}
32	%
33	Its determinant is
34	%
35	\begin{multline}
36	\det \mathbf{F}_n = \Bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\Bigr)\Bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\, e_i^2\Bigr) \\
37	- \Bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\, e_i\Bigr)^2 = W_n^2 \cdot \mathrm{Var}_w(e_i),
38	\end{multline}
39	%
40	where $W_n = \sum_{i,j} w_{nij}$ and $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = \overline{e^2}_n - \bar{e}_n^2$ is the weighted variance of exposure with weights $w_{nij}$. By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) \geq 0$, with equality if and only if $e_i$ is constant across all items with $w_{nij} > 0$. Under conditions~(i) and~(iii), the observed items for model $n$ include at least two distinct exposure values with $w_{nij} > 0$ for the corresponding cells. Therefore $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) > 0$, $\det \mathbf{F}_n > 0$, and each $\mathbf{F}_n$ is positive definite.
41	
42	\paragraph{Step 2: Item and judge blocks.} Under the connected design (condition~i), each item $i$ is observed by at least one $(n,j)$ pair, so the item difficulty block has positive diagonal entries $[\mathbf{F}_{bb}]_{ii} = \sum_{n,j} w_{nij} > 0$ (summing over observed cells). Similarly, each judge $j$ observes at least one $(n,i)$ pair, giving $[\mathbf{F}_{dd}]_{jj} = \sum_{n,i} w_{nij} > 0$. Condition~(ii) ($J \geq 2$) ensures the judge block has sufficient dimension to separate judge effects from the intercept. Both blocks are positive definite.
43	
44	\paragraph{Step 3: Schur complement argument.} The full Fisher information matrix $\mathbf{F}$ contains off-diagonal blocks coupling the parameter groups. Partition as
45	%
46	\begin{equation*}
47	\mathbf{F} = \begin{pmatrix} \mathbf{A} & \mathbf{B} \\ \mathbf{B}^\top & \mathbf{D} \end{pmatrix},
48	\end{equation*}
49	%
50	where $\mathbf{A} = \mathrm{blockdiag}(\mathbf{F}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{F}_N)$ collects all $(\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrices and $\mathbf{D}$ collects the $(\mathbf{b}, \mathbf{d}, \boldsymbol{\tau})$ blocks. From Step~1, $\mathbf{A}$ is positive definite. The Schur complement
51	%
52	\begin{equation}
53	\label{eq:schur}
54	\mathbf{S} = \mathbf{D} - \mathbf{B}^\top \mathbf{A}^{-1} \mathbf{B}
55	\end{equation}
56	%
57	equals the conditional Fisher information for $(\mathbf{b}, \mathbf{d}, \boldsymbol{\tau})$ given $(\boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{c})$. Conditioning on $(\boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{c})$ with fixed $\mathbf{e}$ reduces the model to a standard MFRM with known offsets $c_n \cdot e_i$, whose identifiability is established by \citet[Chapter~5]{sanmartin2016} under conditions~(i), (ii), and~(iv). Therefore $\mathbf{S}$ is positive definite.
58	
59	Since both $\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{S}$ are positive definite:
60	%
61	\begin{equation*}
62	\det \mathbf{F} = \det \mathbf{A} \cdot \det \mathbf{S} > 0,
63	\end{equation*}
64	%
65	establishing non-singularity of $\mathbf{F}$ and hence local identifiability. \qed
66	
67	\paragraph{Local vs.\ global identifiability.} Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient} establishes \emph{local} identifiability: the Fisher information matrix is non-singular at the true parameter value, guaranteeing that no infinitesimally close parameter vector produces the same likelihood. This does not rule out discrete global non-identifiabilities (i.e., isolated points in parameter space with identical likelihood). However, the HalfNormal prior on $c_n$ restricts the parameter space to $c_n \geq 0$, eliminating the sign ambiguity that would otherwise create a global reflection $(\theta_n + \delta,\, c_n - \delta/e_i)$. Combined with the location constraint $\sum_n \theta_n = 0$ and the empirical observation that the posterior is unimodal across all chains (no mode-switching detected), the local result provides a practical guarantee of unique parameter recovery.
68	
69	\paragraph{Role of the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality.} The key step in Step~1 is the decomposition $\det \mathbf{F}_n = W_n^2 \cdot \mathrm{Var}_w(e_i)$, where the weighted variance $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = \mathbb{E}_w[e_i^2] - (\mathbb{E}_w[e_i])^2 \geq 0$ by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality applied to the vectors $(\sqrt{w_{nij}})$ and $(\sqrt{w_{nij}}\, e_i)$. Strict positivity follows from the requirement that $e_i$ takes at least two distinct values over observed items (condition~iii): if $e_i = e$ for all $i$ with $w_{nij} > 0$, then $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = 0$ and the $(\theta_n, c_n)$ block becomes singular, recovering Proposition~\ref{prop:exposure_variation}. The Cauchy--Schwarz bound is tight only in this degenerate case, so any non-trivial exposure variation suffices for identification.
70	
71	
72	\section{Explicit Form of the OVB Coefficient $\gamma_n$}
73	\label{app:gamma}
74	
75	Under the MFRM logistic link, a first-order Taylor expansion of Observation~\ref{obs:ovb} yields an explicit approximation for the bias coefficient $\gamma_n$:
76	%
77	\begin{equation}
78	\label{eq:gamma_explicit}
79	\gamma_n \approx \frac{\sum_{i} w_{ni} \cdot e_i \cdot p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})}{\sum_{i} w_{ni} \cdot p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})},
80	\end{equation}
81	%
82	where $p_{ni} = \sigma(\theta_n - b_i - \bar{d})$ is the MFRM predicted probability averaged over judges and $w_{ni}$ is the effective item weight (number of judge observations for item $i$ on model $n$). Eq.~\ref{eq:gamma_explicit} is a weighted mean of the exposure values $e_i$, with weights proportional to the Fisher information $p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})$. Items near the boundary ($p_{ni} \approx 0.5$) contribute more to the bias than items at the extremes, because the logistic link is most sensitive at these points. This approximation is accurate to first order in $(c_n \cdot e_i)$; the synthetic validation in Section~\ref{sec:confounding} ($r = 0.86$) confirms that the linear approximation captures the dominant bias structure.
83	
84	
85	\section{Murphy--Topel Variance Correction}
86	\label{app:murphy_topel}
87	
88	The three-stage G-MFRM procedure treats Stage~2 exposure estimates $\hat{e}_i$ as fixed in Stage~3, understating posterior uncertainty by ignoring estimation error in $\hat{e}_i$. We derive the Murphy--Topel correction \citep{murphy1985} adapted to the G-MFRM setting.
89	
90	\paragraph{Setup.} Let $\boldsymbol{\alpha} = (e_1, \ldots, e_I)$ denote the Stage~2 parameters estimated from an independent dataset with log-likelihood $\ell_2(\boldsymbol{\alpha})$, and let $\boldsymbol{\psi} = (\boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{c}, \mathbf{b}, \mathbf{d}, \boldsymbol{\tau})$ denote the Stage~3 parameters estimated with log-likelihood $\ell_3(\boldsymbol{\psi}; \hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})$, where $\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}$ is plugged in as known.
91	
92	\paragraph{Naive and Stage~2 variances.} The naive (uncorrected) variance of $\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}$ is
93	%
94	\begin{equation}
95	\mathbf{V}_3 = \left[-\frac{\partial^2 \ell_3}{\partial \boldsymbol{\psi}\, \partial \boldsymbol{\psi}^\top}\bigg|_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\, \hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\right]^{-1},
96	\end{equation}
97	%
98	and the Stage~2 variance is
99	%
100	\begin{equation}
101	\mathbf{V}_2 = \left[-\frac{\partial^2 \ell_2}{\partial \boldsymbol{\alpha}\, \partial \boldsymbol{\alpha}^\top}\bigg|_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\right]^{-1}.
102	\end{equation}
103	
104	\paragraph{Cross-derivative matrix.} The coupling between stages is captured by the cross-derivative
105	%
106	\begin{equation}
107	\mathbf{C} = \frac{\partial^2 \ell_3}{\partial \boldsymbol{\psi}\, \partial \boldsymbol{\alpha}^\top}\bigg|_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\, \hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}.
108	\end{equation}
109	%
110	In the G-MFRM, non-zero entries of $\mathbf{C}$ arise from the contamination gating term $c_n \cdot e_i$. The key entry is
111	%
112	\begin{equation}
113	\frac{\partial^2 \ell_3}{\partial c_n\, \partial e_i} = \sum_{j=1}^{J} \frac{\partial^2 \ell_{nij}}{\partial \eta_{nij}^2} \cdot c_n,
114	\end{equation}
115	%
116	which is proportional to $c_n$: models with zero contamination contribute no cross-derivative. Similarly, $\partial^2 \ell_3 / (\partial \theta_n\, \partial e_i)$ involves only indirect coupling through the shared item observations. The correction is therefore concentrated on the contamination parameters of models with $c_n > 0$.
117	
118	\paragraph{Corrected variance.} The Murphy--Topel corrected variance is
119	%
120	\begin{equation}
121	\label{eq:murphy_topel}
122	\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{MT}} = \mathbf{V}_3 + \mathbf{V}_3\, \mathbf{C}\, \mathbf{V}_2\, \mathbf{C}^\top\, \mathbf{V}_3.
123	\end{equation}
124	%
125	The correction term $\mathbf{V}_3 \mathbf{C} \mathbf{V}_2 \mathbf{C}^\top \mathbf{V}_3$ is positive semi-definite, so $\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{MT}} \succeq \mathbf{V}_3$: the corrected variance is always at least as large as the naive variance. The inflation is concentrated on $c_n$ for contaminated models and is negligible for $\theta_n$ when contamination is sparse, consistent with the empirical pattern in Table~\ref{tab:error_prop}.
126	
127	\paragraph{Implementation.} We estimate $\mathbf{V}_2$ from the Stage~2 bootstrap and compute $\mathbf{C}$ via finite differences of the Stage~3 log-likelihood evaluated at perturbed $\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}$ values. Corrected credible intervals are obtained by inflating Stage~3 posterior samples by $\sqrt{\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{MT}}) / \mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{V}_3)}$.
128	
129	
130	\section{Ablation Study}
131	\label{app:ablation}
132	
133	\begin{table}[h]
134	\centering
135	\scriptsize
136	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
137	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
138	\toprule
139	\textbf{Variant} & $\theta$-RMSE & $\tau$ & $c_n$-RMSE \\
140	\midrule
141	Full G-MFRM & 0.124 $\pm$ 0.016 & 0.922 $\pm$ 0.010 & 0.593 $\pm$ 0.224 \\
142	$-$ Judge severity ($d_j$) & 0.127 $\pm$ 0.016 & 0.922 $\pm$ 0.010 & 0.629 $\pm$ 0.243 \\
143	Binary gating & 0.137 $\pm$ 0.016 & 0.909 $\pm$ 0.014 & 0.833 $\pm$ 0.226 \\
144	Staged estimation & 0.254 $\pm$ 0.045 & 0.839 $\pm$ 0.035 & 1.382 $\pm$ 0.164 \\
145	\bottomrule
146	\end{tabular}
147	\caption{Ablation study on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds). Each row removes or modifies one component of the full G-MFRM. Staged estimation refers to fitting MFRM and contamination sequentially rather than jointly. All differences from Full G-MFRM are significant at $p < 0.001$ (Wilcoxon) except $-d_j$ on $\theta$-RMSE ($p = 0.245$) and $\tau$ ($p = 0.344$).}
148	\label{tab:ablation}
149	\end{table}
150	
151	The ablation confirms that joint estimation is the primary contributor to G-MFRM's advantage: staged estimation increases $\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\times$ (from 0.124 to 0.254, $p < 0.001$) and $c_n$-RMSE by $2.3\times$ (from 0.593 to 1.382, $p < 0.001$), with $\tau$ dropping from 0.922 to 0.839. This degradation directly reflects the omitted variable bias characterized in Section~\ref{sec:confounding}: when contamination and judge effects are estimated sequentially, the first-stage estimates absorb the unmodeled facet.
152	
153	Replacing continuous exposure $e_i \in [0,1]$ with binary gating ($e_i \in \{0, 1\}$) increases $c_n$-RMSE by 40\% (from 0.593 to 0.833, $p < 0.001$), indicating that graded exposure modeling captures contamination magnitude more precisely than a binary flag. The effect on $\theta$-RMSE is moderate ($+$10\%, $p < 0.001$), suggesting that the binary approximation still captures the dominant contamination signal but loses precision on partially exposed items.
154	
155	Removing judge severity $d_j$ does not significantly affect ability estimates ($\theta$-RMSE $+$2.4\%, $p = 0.245$; $\tau$ identical, $p = 0.344$) but significantly degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6.1\%, $p < 0.001$). This confirms that $d_j$ serves as a diagnostic component that improves contamination estimation precision rather than ranking accuracy: without explicit judge severity modeling, judge-induced score variation is partially absorbed into the contamination estimates, inflating $c_n$-RMSE. The $d_j$ profile also provides independent diagnostic value for judge calibration (e.g., Claude Opus $d_j = +0.60$ vs.\ Mistral $-0.74$ in the real-data evaluation).
156

161	\label{fig:ablation_tau}
162	\end{figure}

218	\section{Self-Evaluation Robustness}
219	\label{app:self_eval}
220	
221	Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---appear as both judge and target in our evaluation. Self-judging introduces a potential confound: a model may score its own responses more favorably. We examine the empirical magnitude of this effect and its impact on rankings.
222	
223	Qwen2.5-72B achieves a perfect self-judging win rate of 1.000 (42/42 items), compared to a cross-judge mean win rate of 0.714 for the same model. LLaMA-3.3-70B shows a self-judging win rate of 0.912, compared to a cross-judge mean of 0.823. Both values are elevated relative to cross-judge baselines, consistent with same-family preference leakage \citep{preference_leakage2026}.
224	
225	To assess sensitivity, we re-fit G-MFRM excluding all 99 self-judging observations (Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B judging their own responses). The resulting ranking yields $\tau = 0.857$, identical to the full-data G-MFRM point estimate, with a swap at ranks 2--3. Contamination susceptibility estimates $c_n$ shift by less than 0.15 for all models. This confirms that $d_j$ absorbs the bulk of self-evaluation bias and that rankings are robust to excluding self-judging pairs.
226

256	\section{Judge Count Sensitivity}
257	\label{app:judge_count}
258	
259	Table~\ref{tab:judge_count} reports ranking accuracy as a function of the number of judges $k$, obtained by subsampling from the full 9-judge panel (10 random subsets per $k$; $k = 9$ uses the full panel). G-MFRM outperforms both raw averaging and sequential estimation at all judge counts. Rank agreement increases monotonically with $k$ ($\tau = 0.771 \to 0.857$), and variance contracts as additional judges improve $d_j$ and $c_n$ estimation. At $k \geq 4$, G-MFRM exceeds the full-panel baseline $\tau = 0.786$, suggesting that four diverse judges suffice for practical deployment.

262	\centering

264	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
265	\toprule
266	$k$ & Raw Avg & Sequential & G-MFRM & $\Delta\tau$ \\
267	\midrule
268	2 & $.755 \pm .088$ & $.764 \pm .096$ & $.771 \pm .081$ & $+.017$ \\
269	4 & $.779 \pm .041$ & $.786 \pm .048$ & $.800 \pm .045$ & $+.021$ \\
270	6 & $.800 \pm .030$ & $.807 \pm .048$ & $.821 \pm .051$ & $+.021$ \\
271	9 & $.786$ & $.786$ & $.857$ & $+.071$ \\
272	\bottomrule
