  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}\nLLM-as-a-judge evaluations are subject to multiple systematic biases. \\citet{wang2024positionbias} demonstrate that LLM judges exhibit strong position bias in pairwise evaluation, where swapping the order of candidate responses can flip the ranking; \\citet{li2024splitmerge} propose a split-and-merge strategy to align position biases across evaluators. Beyond positional effects, \\citet{koo2024cobbler} benchmark six cognitive biases in 15 LLM evaluators, finding significant bias in approximately 40\\% of comparisons, and \\citet{ye2024justice} quantify verbosity and self-enhancement biases. \\citet{panickssery2024selffavor} show that LLMs systematically favor their own generations, a confound that affects any evaluation where a judge model shares a family with a target. On the correction side, CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias via round-robin assignment but treats residual variation as genuine ability. Unsupervised Debiasing Alignment (UDA) \\citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\\% without modeling contamination. \\citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty} formalize judge variability via $J/\\Delta J$ diagnostics. \\citet{bavaresco2025judgebench} evaluate 11 LLMs as judges across 20 NLP tasks, finding large variance across datasets and emphasizing the need for task-specific validation. All of these methods model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination effects into ability estimates (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}\nLLM-as-a-judge evaluations exhibit position bias \\citep{wang2024positionbias}, self-preference \\citep{panickssery2024selffavor}, and other cognitive biases \\citep{koo2024cobbler, ye2024justice, bavaresco2025judgebench}; see also \\citet{li2024splitmerge} and \\citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty}. On the correction side, CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias via round-robin assignment, and UDA \\citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\\%. All of these methods model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination effects into ability estimates (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding})."
}

All of these methods model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination effects into ability estimates (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}\nLLM-as-a-judge evaluations are subject to multiple systematic biases. \\citet{wang2024positionbias} demonstrate that LLM judges exhibit strong position bias in pairwise evaluation, where swapping the order of candidate responses can flip the ranking; \\citet{li2024splitmerge} propose a split-and-merge strategy to align position biases across evaluators. Beyond positional effects, \\citet{koo2024cobbler} benchmark six cognitive biases in 15 LLM evaluators, finding significant bias in approximately 40\\% of comparisons, and \\citet{ye2024justice} quantify verbosity and self-enhancement biases. \\citet{panickssery2024selffavor} show that LLMs systematically favor their own generations, a confound that affects any evaluation where a judge model shares a family with a target. On the correction side, CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias via round-robin assignment but treats residual variation as genuine ability. Unsupervised Debiasing Alignment (UDA) \\citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\\% without modeling contamination. \\citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty} formalize judge variability via $J/\\Delta J$ diagnostics. \\citet{bavaresco2025judgebench} evaluate 11 LLMs as judges across 20 NLP tasks, finding large variance across datasets and emphasizing the need for task-specific validation. All of these methods model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination effects into ability estimates (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Contamination Detection and Avoidance.}\nBenchmark contamination arises when evaluation items appear in training data, inflating performance estimates. Detection methods take multiple approaches: ConStat \\citep{constat2024} uses performance-based statistical tests comparing original and rephrased items; Data Contamination Risk (DCR) \\citep{dcr2025} quantifies contamination via a unified metric integrating multiple evidence sources; LogProber \\citep{logprober2024} leverages token-level log-probabilities to disentangle memorization from genuine capability; and Min-K\\% \\citep{mink2024} detects pretraining data membership through outlier token probabilities. \\citet{golchin2024timetravel} propose guided instruction prompting to detect instance-level contamination, while \\citet{oren2024proving} provide provable statistical guarantees for black-box contamination detection. \\citet{deng2024contamination} and \\citet{sainz2023contamination} investigate the prevalence and measurement of contamination across modern benchmarks. On the judge side, \\citet{preference_leakage2026} identify preference leakage, where judge-side familiarity with benchmark items inflates scores---a complementary phenomenon to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus. Contamination avoidance strategies include dynamic benchmarks such as LiveBench \\citep{livebench2025}, private evaluation via TRUCE \\citep{truce2024}, and efficient subset selection via tinyBenchmarks \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks}. G-MFRM takes a complementary approach: rather than preventing contamination, it corrects for residual contamination in existing benchmarks while simultaneously modeling judge effects.\n\n\\paragraph{Item Response Theory (IRT) and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}\nItem Response Theory provides a principled measurement framework for jointly modeling item difficulty and examinee ability. \\citet{lalor2019irt} demonstrate that IRT item parameters can be learned from model ensembles (``artificial crowds'') without human responses. \\citet{rodriguez2021irt} apply IRT to NLP leaderboards, showing that IRT-based rankings can identify annotation errors and highlight informative evaluation items. \\citet{vania2021irt} use IRT to compare 29 NLP datasets across 18 models, revealing which benchmarks are saturated versus discriminative. In the multi-rater setting, the Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} extends IRT to separate rater effects from examinee ability. \\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply MFRM to compare ten LLM raters with human experts, demonstrating that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects. \\citet{uto2020generalized} propose a generalized MFRM with Bayesian estimation; \\citet{choi2026irt_judge} diagnose LLM judge reliability via IRT. Pseudo-Siamese Network for IRT (PSN-IRT) \\citep{zhou2025psnirt} extends IRT with neural architectures but does not include contamination facets. The Doubly-Gated IRT (DG-IRT) model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt} is the closest antecedent, introducing binary gating for item preknowledge in educational testing; G-MFRM extends this to continuous exposure, multi-rater designs, and provides formal identifiability analysis not available for DG-IRT (Section~\\ref{sec:model_spec}).\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

          "-LLM-as-a-judge evaluations are subject to multiple systematic biases. \\citet{wang2024positionbias} demonstrate that LLM judges exhibit strong position bias in pairwise evaluation, where swapping the order of candidate responses can flip the ranking; \\citet{li2024splitmerge} propose a split-and-merge strategy to align position biases across evaluators. Beyond positional effects, \\citet{koo2024cobbler} benchmark six cognitive biases in 15 LLM evaluators, finding significant bias in approximately 40\\% of comparisons, and \\citet{ye2024justice} quantify verbosity and self-enhancement biases. \\citet{panickssery2024selffavor} show that LLMs systematically favor their own generations, a confound that affects any evaluation where a judge model shares a family with a target. On the correction side, CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias via round-robin assignment but treats residual variation as genuine ability. Unsupervised Debiasing Alignment (UDA) \\citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\\% without modeling contamination. \\citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty} formalize judge variability via $J/\\Delta J$ diagnostics. \\citet{bavaresco2025judgebench} evaluate 11 LLMs as judges across 20 NLP tasks, finding large variance across datasets and emphasizing the need for task-specific validation. All of these methods model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination effects into ability estimates (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).",
          "+LLM-as-a-judge evaluations exhibit position bias \\citep{wang2024positionbias}, self-preference \\citep{panickssery2024selffavor}, and other cognitive biases \\citep{koo2024cobbler, ye2024justice, bavaresco2025judgebench}; see also \\citet{li2024splitmerge} and \\citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty}. On the correction side, CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias via round-robin assignment, and UDA \\citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\\%. All of these methods model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination effects into ability estimates (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).",

Detection methods take multiple approaches: ConStat \\citep{constat2024} uses performance-based statistical tests comparing original and rephrased items; Data Contamination Risk (DCR) \\citep{dcr2025} quantifies contamination via a unified metric integrating multiple evidence sources; LogProber \\citep{logprober2024} leverages token-level log-probabilities to disentangle memorization from genuine capability; and Min-K\\% \\citep{mink2024} detects pretraining data membership through outlier token probabilities. \\citet{golchin2024timetravel} propose guided instruction prompting to detect instance-level contamination, while \\citet{oren2024proving} provide provable statistical guarantees for black-box contamination detection. \\citet{deng2024contamination} and \\citet{sainz2023contamination} investigate the prevalence and measurement of contamination across modern benchmarks. On the judge side, \\citet{preference_leakage2026} identify preference leakage, where judge-side familiarity with benchmark items inflates scores---a complementary phenomenon to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus. Contamination avoidance strategies include dynamic benchmarks such as LiveBench \\citep{livebench2025}, private evaluation via TRUCE \\citep{truce2024}, and efficient subset selection via tinyBenchmarks \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks}. G-MFRM takes a complementary approach: rather than preventing contamination, it corrects for residual contamination in existing benchmarks while simultaneously modeling judge effects."

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Benchmark Contamination Detection and Avoidance.}\nBenchmark contamination arises when evaluation items appear in training data, inflating performance estimates. Detection methods take multiple approaches: ConStat \\citep{constat2024} uses performance-based statistical tests comparing original and rephrased items; Data Contamination Risk (DCR) \\citep{dcr2025} quantifies contamination via a unified metric integrating multiple evidence sources; LogProber \\citep{logprober2024} leverages token-level log-probabilities to disentangle memorization from genuine capability; and Min-K\\% \\citep{mink2024} detects pretraining data membership through outlier token probabilities. \\citet{golchin2024timetravel} propose guided instruction prompting to detect instance-level contamination, while \\citet{oren2024proving} provide provable statistical guarantees for black-box contamination detection. \\citet{deng2024contamination} and \\citet{sainz2023contamination} investigate the prevalence and measurement of contamination across modern benchmarks. On the judge side, \\citet{preference_leakage2026} identify preference leakage, where judge-side familiarity with benchmark items inflates scores---a complementary phenomenon to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus. Contamination avoidance strategies include dynamic benchmarks such as LiveBench \\citep{livebench2025}, private evaluation via TRUCE \\citep{truce2024}, and efficient subset selection via tinyBenchmarks \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks}. G-MFRM takes a complementary approach: rather than preventing contamination, it corrects for residual contamination in existing benchmarks while simultaneously modeling judge effects.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Benchmark Contamination Detection and Avoidance.}\nDetection methods include performance-based tests \\citep{constat2024}, unified contamination metrics \\citep{dcr2025}, token-level log-probability probes \\citep{logprober2024, mink2024}, and guided prompting \\citep{golchin2024timetravel, oren2024proving}; see also \\citet{deng2024contamination, sainz2023contamination}. \\citet{preference_leakage2026} identify judge-side preference leakage, complementary to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus. Avoidance strategies include dynamic benchmarks \\citep{livebench2025}, private evaluation \\citep{truce2024}, and subset selection \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks}. G-MFRM instead corrects for residual contamination while simultaneously modeling judge effects.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}\nLLM-as-a-judge evaluations exhibit position bias \\citep{wang2024positionbias}, self-preference \\citep{panickssery2024selffavor}, and other cognitive biases \\citep{koo2024cobbler, ye2024justice, bavaresco2025judgebench}; see also \\citet{li2024splitmerge} and \\citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty}. On the correction side, CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias via round-robin assignment, and UDA \\citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\\%. All of these methods model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination effects into ability estimates (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Contamination Detection and Avoidance.}\nBenchmark contamination arises when evaluation items appear in training data, inflating performance estimates. Detection methods take multiple approaches: ConStat \\citep{constat2024} uses performance-based statistical tests comparing original and rephrased items; Data Contamination Risk (DCR) \\citep{dcr2025} quantifies contamination via a unified metric integrating multiple evidence sources; LogProber \\citep{logprober2024} leverages token-level log-probabilities to disentangle memorization from genuine capability; and Min-K\\% \\citep{mink2024} detects pretraining data membership through outlier token probabilities. \\citet{golchin2024timetravel} propose guided instruction prompting to detect instance-level contamination, while \\citet{oren2024proving} provide provable statistical guarantees for black-box contamination detection. \\citet{deng2024contamination} and \\citet{sainz2023contamination} investigate the prevalence and measurement of contamination across modern benchmarks. On the judge side, \\citet{preference_leakage2026} identify preference leakage, where judge-side familiarity with benchmark items inflates scores---a complementary phenomenon to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus. Contamination avoidance strategies include dynamic benchmarks such as LiveBench \\citep{livebench2025}, private evaluation via TRUCE \\citep{truce2024}, and efficient subset selection via tinyBenchmarks \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks}. G-MFRM takes a complementary approach: rather than preventing contamination, it corrects for residual contamination in existing benchmarks while simultaneously modeling judge effects.\n\n\\paragraph{Item Response Theory (IRT) and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}\nItem Response Theory provides a principled measurement framework for jointly modeling item difficulty and examinee ability. \\citet{lalor2019irt} demonstrate that IRT item parameters can be learned from model ensembles (``artificial crowds'') without human responses. \\citet{rodriguez2021irt} apply IRT to NLP leaderboards, showing that IRT-based rankings can identify annotation errors and highlight informative evaluation items. \\citet{vania2021irt} use IRT to compare 29 NLP datasets across 18 models, revealing which benchmarks are saturated versus discriminative. In the multi-rater setting, the Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} extends IRT to separate rater effects from examinee ability. \\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply MFRM to compare ten LLM raters with human experts, demonstrating that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects. \\citet{uto2020generalized} propose a generalized MFRM with Bayesian estimation; \\citet{choi2026irt_judge} diagnose LLM judge reliability via IRT. Pseudo-Siamese Network for IRT (PSN-IRT) \\citep{zhou2025psnirt} extends IRT with neural architectures but does not include contamination facets. The Doubly-Gated IRT (DG-IRT) model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt} is the closest antecedent, introducing binary gating for item preknowledge in educational testing; G-MFRM extends this to continuous exposure, multi-rater designs, and provides formal identifiability analysis not available for DG-IRT (Section~\\ref{sec:model_spec}).\n",

In the multi-rater setting, the Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} extends IRT to separate rater effects from examinee ability. \\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply MFRM to compare ten LLM raters with human experts, demonstrating that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects. \\citet{uto2020generalized} propose a generalized MFRM with Bayesian estimation; \\citet{choi2026irt_judge} diagnose LLM judge reliability via IRT. The Doubly-Gated IRT (DG-IRT) model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt} is the closest antecedent, introducing binary gating for item preknowledge in educational testing; G-MFRM extends this to continuous exposure, multi-rater designs, and provides formal identifiability analysis not available for DG-IRT (Section~\\ref{sec:model_spec})."

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Item Response Theory (IRT) and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}\nItem Response Theory provides a principled measurement framework for jointly modeling item difficulty and examinee ability. \\citet{lalor2019irt} demonstrate that IRT item parameters can be learned from model ensembles (``artificial crowds'') without human responses. \\citet{rodriguez2021irt} apply IRT to NLP leaderboards, showing that IRT-based rankings can identify annotation errors and highlight informative evaluation items. \\citet{vania2021irt} use IRT to compare 29 NLP datasets across 18 models, revealing which benchmarks are saturated versus discriminative. In the multi-rater setting, the Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} extends IRT to separate rater effects from examinee ability. \\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply MFRM to compare ten LLM raters with human experts, demonstrating that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects. \\citet{uto2020generalized} propose a generalized MFRM with Bayesian estimation; \\citet{choi2026irt_judge} diagnose LLM judge reliability via IRT. Pseudo-Siamese Network for IRT (PSN-IRT) \\citep{zhou2025psnirt} extends IRT with neural architectures but does not include contamination facets. The Doubly-Gated IRT (DG-IRT) model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt} is the closest antecedent, introducing binary gating for item preknowledge in educational testing; G-MFRM extends this to continuous exposure, multi-rater designs, and provides formal identifiability analysis not available for DG-IRT (Section~\\ref{sec:model_spec}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Item Response Theory (IRT) and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}\nIRT has been applied to NLP leaderboards for ranking and benchmark analysis \\citep{lalor2019irt, rodriguez2021irt, vania2021irt}. The Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} extends IRT to multi-rater settings; \\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply it to LLM judge panels, and \\citet{uto2020generalized} and \\citet{choi2026irt_judge} propose Bayesian variants; see also \\citet{zhou2025psnirt}. The Doubly-Gated IRT (DG-IRT) model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt} is the closest antecedent, introducing binary gating for item preknowledge; G-MFRM extends this to continuous exposure, multi-rater designs, and formal identifiability analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:model_spec})."
}

    "newString": "\\section{Proofs of Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new} and~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}}\n\\label{app:prop_proofs}\n\n\\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\\ref{prop:no_new}]\nSuppose $c_n' = c_n + \\delta$ preserves all response probabilities. Then $\\delta \\cdot e_i = 0$ for all items. Since $e_i \\neq 0$ for at least one item by assumption, $\\delta = 0$. Fixing $e_i$ externally eliminates the bilinear ambiguity $c_n \\cdot e_i = (\\lambda c_n)(\\lambda^{-1} e_i)$ that would otherwise render the decomposition non-unique.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}]\nWhen exposure is constant, the linear predictor becomes $(\\theta_n + c_n \\cdot e) - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k$, equivalent to a standard MFRM with reparameterized ability $\\theta^*_n = \\theta_n + c_n \\cdot e$. No configuration of response data can distinguish $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ from $(\\theta_n + \\epsilon, c_n - \\epsilon/e)$ for any $\\epsilon$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\n\\section{Proof of Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}}\n\\label{app:proof}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Proof of Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}}\n\\label{app:proof}\n\nWe prove that the G-MFRM is locally identifiable under the conditions of Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}. Following \\citet{rothenberg1971}, a parametric model is locally identifiable at the true parameter vector $\\boldsymbol{\\psi}_0$ if and only if the Fisher information matrix $\\mathbf{F}(\\boldsymbol{\\psi}_0)$ is non-singular.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.} The G-MFRM parameter vector is $\\boldsymbol{\\psi} = (\\boldsymbol{\\theta}, \\mathbf{c}, \\mathbf{b}, \\mathbf{d}, \\boldsymbol{\\tau})$, where $\\boldsymbol{\\theta} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{N-1}$ (after the location constraint $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$), $\\mathbf{c} \\in \\mathbb{R}_{\\geq 0}^N$, $\\mathbf{b} \\in \\mathbb{R}^I$, $\\mathbf{d} \\in \\mathbb{R}^J$, and $\\boldsymbol{\\tau} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{K-1}$. The exposure vector $\\mathbf{e} = (e_1, \\ldots, e_I)$ is fixed externally and treated as known. The linear predictor is $\\eta_{nijk} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i$, with partial derivatives:\n%\n\\begin{align*}\n\\frac{\\partial \\eta_{nijk}}{\\partial \\theta_n} &= 1, \\quad\n\\frac{\\partial \\eta_{nijk}}{\\partial c_n} = e_i, \\quad\n\\frac{\\partial \\eta_{nijk}}{\\partial b_i} = -1, \\\\\n\\frac{\\partial \\eta_{nijk}}{\\partial d_j} &= -1, \\quad\n\\frac{\\partial \\eta_{nijk}}{\\partial \\tau_k} = -1.\n\\end{align*}\n\n\\paragraph{Fisher information weights.} For each observation cell $(n,i,j)$, the Fisher information weight under the cumulative logit model is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:fisher_weight}\nw_{nij} = \\sum_{k=1}^{K} \\frac{\\bigl[\\sigma'(\\eta_{nijk}) - \\sigma'(\\eta_{nij,k+1})\\bigr]^2}{P(Y_{nij} = k)},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $\\sigma(x) = (1 + e^{-x})^{-1}$, $\\sigma'(x) = \\sigma(x)(1 - \\sigma(x))$, and we set $\\sigma'(\\eta_{nij,0}) = \\sigma'(\\eta_{nij,K+1}) = 0$ at the boundaries. Since $P(Y_{nij} = k) > 0$ for all categories and $\\sigma'(\\cdot) > 0$, we have $w_{nij} > 0$ for all $(n,i,j)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Step 1: The $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix.} For each model $n$, the $2 \\times 2$ Fisher information submatrix is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:fisher_block}\n\\mathbf{F}_n = \\sum_{i=1}^{I} \\sum_{j=1}^{J} w_{nij}\n\\begin{pmatrix} 1 & e_i \\\\ e_i & e_i^2 \\end{pmatrix}.\n\\end{equation}\n%\nIts determinant is\n%\n\\begin{multline}\n\\det \\mathbf{F}_n = \\Bigl(\\textstyle\\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\\Bigr)\\Bigl(\\textstyle\\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\\, e_i^2\\Bigr) \\\\\n- \\Bigl(\\textstyle\\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\\, e_i\\Bigr)^2 = W_n^2 \\cdot \\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i),\n\\end{multline}\n%\nwhere $W_n = \\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}$ and $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = \\overline{e^2}_n - \\bar{e}_n^2$ is the weighted variance of exposure with weights $w_{nij}$. By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) \\geq 0$, with equality if and only if $e_i$ is constant across all items with $w_{nij} > 0$. Under conditions~(i) and~(iii), the observed items for model $n$ include at least two distinct exposure values with $w_{nij} > 0$ for the corresponding cells. Therefore $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) > 0$, $\\det \\mathbf{F}_n > 0$, and each $\\mathbf{F}_n$ is positive definite.\n\n\\paragraph{Step 2: Item and judge blocks.} Under the connected design (condition~i), each item $i$ is observed by at least one $(n,j)$ pair, so the item difficulty block has positive diagonal entries $[\\mathbf{F}_{bb}]_{ii} = \\sum_{n,j} w_{nij} > 0$ (summing over observed cells). Similarly, each judge $j$ observes at least one $(n,i)$ pair, giving $[\\mathbf{F}_{dd}]_{jj} = \\sum_{n,i} w_{nij} > 0$. Condition~(ii) ($J \\geq 2$) ensures the judge block has sufficient dimension to separate judge effects from the intercept. Both blocks are positive definite.\n\n\\paragraph{Step 3: Schur complement argument.} The full Fisher information matrix $\\mathbf{F}$ contains off-diagonal blocks coupling the parameter groups. Partition as\n%\n\\begin{equation*}\n\\mathbf{F} = \\begin{pmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^\\top & \\mathbf{D} \\end{pmatrix},\n\\end{equation*}\n%\nwhere $\\mathbf{A} = \\mathrm{blockdiag}(\\mathbf{F}_1, \\ldots, \\mathbf{F}_N)$ collects all $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrices and $\\mathbf{D}$ collects the $(\\mathbf{b}, \\mathbf{d}, \\boldsymbol{\\tau})$ blocks. From Step~1, $\\mathbf{A}$ is positive definite. The Schur complement\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:schur}\n\\mathbf{S} = \\mathbf{D} - \\mathbf{B}^\\top \\mathbf{A}^{-1} \\mathbf{B}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nequals the conditional Fisher information for $(\\mathbf{b}, \\mathbf{d}, \\boldsymbol{\\tau})$ given $(\\boldsymbol{\\theta}, \\mathbf{c})$. Conditioning on $(\\boldsymbol{\\theta}, \\mathbf{c})$ with fixed $\\mathbf{e}$ reduces the model to a standard MFRM with known offsets $c_n \\cdot e_i$, whose identifiability is established by \\citet[Chapter~5]{sanmartin2016} under conditions~(i), (ii), and~(iv). Therefore $\\mathbf{S}$ is positive definite.\n\nSince both $\\mathbf{A}$ and $\\mathbf{S}$ are positive definite:\n%\n\\begin{equation*}\n\\det \\mathbf{F} = \\det \\mathbf{A} \\cdot \\det \\mathbf{S} > 0,\n\\end{equation*}\n%\nestablishing non-singularity of $\\mathbf{F}$ and hence local identifiability. \\qed\n\n\\paragraph{Local vs.\\ global identifiability.} Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient} establishes \\emph{local} identifiability: the Fisher information matrix is non-singular at the true parameter value, guaranteeing that no infinitesimally close parameter vector produces the same likelihood. This does not rule out discrete global non-identifiabilities (i.e., isolated points in parameter space with identical likelihood). However, the HalfNormal prior on $c_n$ restricts the parameter space to $c_n \\geq 0$, eliminating the sign ambiguity that would otherwise create a global reflection $(\\theta_n + \\delta,\\, c_n - \\delta/e_i)$. Combined with the location constraint $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$ and the empirical observation that the posterior is unimodal across all chains (no mode-switching detected), the local result provides a practical guarantee of unique parameter recovery.\n\n\\paragraph{Role of the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality.} The key step in Step~1 is the decomposition $\\det \\mathbf{F}_n = W_n^2 \\cdot \\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i)$, where the weighted variance $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = \\mathbb{E}_w[e_i^2] - (\\mathbb{E}_w[e_i])^2 \\geq 0$ by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality applied to the vectors $(\\sqrt{w_{nij}})$ and $(\\sqrt{w_{nij}}\\, e_i)$. Strict positivity follows from the requirement that $e_i$ takes at least two distinct values over observed items (condition~iii): if $e_i = e$ for all $i$ with $w_{nij} > 0$, then $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = 0$ and the $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ block becomes singular, recovering Proposition~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}. The Cauchy--Schwarz bound is tight only in this degenerate case, so any non-trivial exposure variation suffices for identification.\n\n\n\\section{Explicit Form of the OVB Coefficient $\\gamma_n$}\n\\label{app:gamma}\n\nUnder the MFRM logistic link, a first-order Taylor expansion of Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb} yields an explicit approximation for the bias coefficient $\\gamma_n$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:gamma_explicit}\n\\gamma_n \\approx \\frac{\\sum_{i} w_{ni} \\cdot e_i \\cdot p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})}{\\sum_{i} w_{ni} \\cdot p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $p_{ni} = \\sigma(\\theta_n - b_i - \\bar{d})$ is the MFRM predicted probability averaged over judges and $w_{ni}$ is the effective item weight (number of judge observations for item $i$ on model $n$). Eq.~\\ref{eq:gamma_explicit} is a weighted mean of the exposure values $e_i$, with weights proportional to the Fisher information $p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})$. Items near the boundary ($p_{ni} \\approx 0.5$) contribute more to the bias than items at the extremes, because the logistic link is most sensitive at these points. This approximation is accurate to first order in $(c_n \\cdot e_i)$; the synthetic validation in Section~\\ref{sec:confounding} ($r = 0.86$) confirms that the linear approximation captures the dominant bias structure.\n\n\n\\section{Murphy--Topel Variance Correction}\n\\label{app:murphy_topel}\n\nThe three-stage G-MFRM procedure treats Stage~2 exposure estimates $\\hat{e}_i$ as fixed in Stage~3, understating posterior uncertainty by ignoring estimation error in $\\hat{e}_i$. We derive the Murphy--Topel correction \\citep{murphy1985} adapted to the G-MFRM setting.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.} Let $\\boldsymbol{\\alpha} = (e_1, \\ldots, e_I)$ denote the Stage~2 parameters estimated from an independent dataset with log-likelihood $\\ell_2(\\boldsymbol{\\alpha})$, and let $\\boldsymbol{\\psi} = (\\boldsymbol{\\theta}, \\mathbf{c}, \\mathbf{b}, \\mathbf{d}, \\boldsymbol{\\tau})$ denote the Stage~3 parameters estimated with log-likelihood $\\ell_3(\\boldsymbol{\\psi}; \\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}})$, where $\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}}$ is plugged in as known.\n\n\\paragraph{Naive and Stage~2 variances.} The naive (uncorrected) variance of $\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\psi}}$ is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathbf{V}_3 = \\left[-\\frac{\\partial^2 \\ell_3}{\\partial \\boldsymbol{\\psi}\\, \\partial \\boldsymbol{\\psi}^\\top}\\bigg|_{\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\psi}},\\, \\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}}}\\right]^{-1},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nand the Stage~2 variance is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathbf{V}_2 = \\left[-\\frac{\\partial^2 \\ell_2}{\\partial \\boldsymbol{\\alpha}\\, \\partial \\boldsymbol{\\alpha}^\\top}\\bigg|_{\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}}}\\right]^{-1}.\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-derivative matrix.} The coupling between stages is captured by the cross-derivative\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathbf{C} = \\frac{\\partial^2 \\ell_3}{\\partial \\boldsymbol{\\psi}\\, \\partial \\boldsymbol{\\alpha}^\\top}\\bigg|_{\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\psi}},\\, \\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}}}.\n\\end{equation}\n%\nIn the G-MFRM, non-zero entries of $\\mathbf{C}$ arise from the contamination gating term $c_n \\cdot e_i$. The key entry is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\frac{\\partial^2 \\ell_3}{\\partial c_n\\, \\partial e_i} = \\sum_{j=1}^{J} \\frac{\\partial^2 \\ell_{nij}}{\\partial \\eta_{nij}^2} \\cdot c_n,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhich is proportional to $c_n$: models with zero contamination contribute no cross-derivative. Similarly, $\\partial^2 \\ell_3 / (\\partial \\theta_n\\, \\partial e_i)$ involves only indirect coupling through the shared item observations. The correction is therefore concentrated on the contamination parameters of models with $c_n > 0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Corrected variance.} The Murphy--Topel corrected variance is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:murphy_topel}\n\\mathbf{V}_{\\mathrm{MT}} = \\mathbf{V}_3 + \\mathbf{V}_3\\, \\mathbf{C}\\, \\mathbf{V}_2\\, \\mathbf{C}^\\top\\, \\mathbf{V}_3.\n\\end{equation}\n%\nThe correction term $\\mathbf{V}_3 \\mathbf{C} \\mathbf{V}_2 \\mathbf{C}^\\top \\mathbf{V}_3$ is positive semi-definite, so $\\mathbf{V}_{\\mathrm{MT}} \\succeq \\mathbf{V}_3$: the corrected variance is always at least as large as the naive variance. The inflation is concentrated on $c_n$ for contaminated models and is negligible for $\\theta_n$ when contamination is sparse, consistent with the empirical pattern in Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}.\n\n\\paragraph{Implementation.} We estimate $\\mathbf{V}_2$ from the Stage~2 bootstrap and compute $\\mathbf{C}$ via finite differences of the Stage~3 log-likelihood evaluated at perturbed $\\hat{\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}}$ values. Corrected credible intervals are obtained by inflating Stage~3 posterior samples by $\\sqrt{\\mathrm{diag}(\\mathbf{V}_{\\mathrm{MT}}) / \\mathrm{diag}(\\mathbf{V}_3)}$.\n\n\n\\section{Ablation Study}\n\\label{app:ablation}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & $\\theta$-RMSE & $\\tau$ & $c_n$-RMSE \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull G-MFRM & 0.124 $\\pm$ 0.016 & 0.922 $\\pm$ 0.010 & 0.593 $\\pm$ 0.224 \\\\\n$-$ Judge severity ($d_j$) & 0.127 $\\pm$ 0.016 & 0.922 $\\pm$ 0.010 & 0.629 $\\pm$ 0.243 \\\\\nBinary gating & 0.137 $\\pm$ 0.016 & 0.909 $\\pm$ 0.014 & 0.833 $\\pm$ 0.226 \\\\\nStaged estimation & 0.254 $\\pm$ 0.045 & 0.839 $\\pm$ 0.035 & 1.382 $\\pm$ 0.164 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Ablation study on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds). Each row removes or modifies one component of the full G-MFRM. Staged estimation refers to fitting MFRM and contamination sequentially rather than jointly. All differences from Full G-MFRM are significant at $p < 0.001$ (Wilcoxon) except $-d_j$ on $\\theta$-RMSE ($p = 0.245$) and $\\tau$ ($p = 0.344$).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe ablation confirms that joint estimation is the primary contributor to G-MFRM's advantage: staged estimation increases $\\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\\times$ (from 0.124 to 0.254, $p < 0.001$) and $c_n$-RMSE by $2.3\\times$ (from 0.593 to 1.382, $p < 0.001$), with $\\tau$ dropping from 0.922 to 0.839. This degradation directly reflects the omitted variable bias characterized in Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}: when contamination and judge effects are estimated sequentially, the first-stage estimates absorb the unmodeled facet.\n\nReplacing continuous exposure $e_i \\in [0,1]$ with binary gating ($e_i \\in \\{0, 1\\}$) increases $c_n$-RMSE by 40\\% (from 0.593 to 0.833, $p < 0.001$), indicating that graded exposure modeling captures contamination magnitude more precisely than a binary flag. The effect on $\\theta$-RMSE is moderate ($+$10\\%, $p < 0.001$), suggesting that the binary approximation still captures the dominant contamination signal but loses precision on partially exposed items.\n\nRemoving judge severity $d_j$ does not significantly affect ability estimates ($\\theta$-RMSE $+$2.4\\%, $p = 0.245$; $\\tau$ identical, $p = 0.344$) but significantly degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6.1\\%, $p < 0.001$). This confirms that $d_j$ serves as a diagnostic component that improves contamination estimation precision rather than ranking accuracy: without explicit judge severity modeling, judge-induced score variation is partially absorbed into the contamination estimates, inflating $c_n$-RMSE. The $d_j$ profile also provides independent diagnostic value for judge calibration (e.g., Claude Opus $d_j = +0.60$ vs.\\ Mistral $-0.74$ in the real-data evaluation).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{theta_tau_boxplot}\n\\caption{Ranking accuracy (Kendall $\\tau$ with ground truth) across ablation variants. Full G-MFRM achieves the highest and most consistent $\\tau$; staged estimation shows the largest degradation and highest variance. Synthetic data: $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds.}\n\\label{fig:ablation_tau}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Posterior Predictive Check}\n\\label{app:ppc}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:ppc} shows the posterior predictive distribution from 1{,}000 posterior draws over 5{,}661 observations. The predicted and observed response distributions are well-aligned (predicted mean win rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$), with no systematic discrepancy across item difficulty or model ability levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ppc}\n\\caption{Posterior predictive check. Distribution of predicted (gray) and observed (black) response rates across all model--item--judge triplets. Mean predicted $= 0.593$, mean observed $= 0.599$.}\n\\label{fig:ppc}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{LOO Model Comparison}\n\\label{app:loo_comparison}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:model_comparison} visualizes the LOO-ELPD difference between G-MFRM and MFRM-only. G-MFRM is the reference model ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD} = 0$); MFRM-only shows a deficit of $-55.7$ (SE $= 9.4$), confirming that the contamination gating term substantially improves held-out predictive fit.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{model_comparison}\n\\caption{LOO-ELPD model comparison. G-MFRM (reference) versus MFRM-only. Error bars show $\\pm$1 SE. The $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$ strongly favors G-MFRM.}\n\\label{fig:model_comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:pareto_k} shows the Pareto $\\hat{k}$ diagnostic values for both models. All observations have $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming that the LOO approximation is reliable for both models.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{pareto_k_plot}\n\\caption{Pareto $\\hat{k}$ diagnostics for G-MFRM (left) and MFRM-only (right). All 5{,}661 observations fall below the $\\hat{k} = 0.7$ threshold, confirming reliable LOO estimates.}\n\\label{fig:pareto_k}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{OVB Validation Across Confounding Levels}\n\\label{app:ovb_validation}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho} (main text) shows that sequential estimator bias remains approximately constant across confounding levels ($\\rho$), confirming omission-driven rather than confounding-strength-dependent bias.\n\n\n\\section{Contamination Susceptibility Recovery}\n\\label{app:cn_recovery}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:cn_recovery} (main text) shows contamination susceptibility recovery from 50 Monte Carlo seeds.\n\n\n\\section{Error Propagation Details}\n\\label{app:error_prop_fig}\n\nThe error propagation figure is presented in the main text (Figure~\\ref{fig:error_prop}). See Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop} for numerical values.\n\n\n\\section{Self-Evaluation Robustness}\n\\label{app:self_eval}\n\nTwo models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---appear as both judge and target in our evaluation. Self-judging introduces a potential confound: a model may score its own responses more favorably. We examine the empirical magnitude of this effect and its impact on rankings.\n\nQwen2.5-72B achieves a perfect self-judging win rate of 1.000 (42/42 items), compared to a cross-judge mean win rate of 0.714 for the same model. LLaMA-3.3-70B shows a self-judging win rate of 0.912, compared to a cross-judge mean of 0.823. Both values are elevated relative to cross-judge baselines, consistent with same-family preference leakage \\citep{preference_leakage2026}.\n\nTo assess sensitivity, we re-fit G-MFRM excluding all 99 self-judging observations (Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B judging their own responses). The resulting ranking yields $\\tau = 0.857$, identical to the full-data G-MFRM point estimate, with a swap at ranks 2--3. Contamination susceptibility estimates $c_n$ shift by less than 0.15 for all models. This confirms that $d_j$ absorbs the bulk of self-evaluation bias and that rankings are robust to excluding self-judging pairs.\n\n\n\\section{Judge Severity Profiles}\n\\label{app:judge_severity}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:judge_severity} reports posterior estimates of judge severity $d_j$ for all nine judges. Positive values indicate stricter scoring (lower win rates); negative values indicate more lenient scoring. Proprietary models cluster at the strict end, while open-source models tend to be more lenient.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Judge} & $\\hat{d}_j$ & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\nClaude Opus 4.7 & $+0.60$ & [$+0.43$, $+0.76$] \\\\\nGPT-5.5 & $+0.53$ & [$+0.37$, $+0.70$] \\\\\nDeepSeek-V4-Pro & $+0.31$ & [$+0.07$, $+0.54$] \\\\\nClaude Sonnet 4.6 & $+0.14$ & [$-0.04$, $+0.31$] \\\\\nGemini-2.5-Pro & $-0.04$ & [$-0.27$, $+0.19$] \\\\\nQwen3-235B & $-0.04$ & [$-0.24$, $+0.16$] \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B & $-0.35$ & [$-0.64$, $-0.07$] \\\\\nLLaMA-3.3-70B & $-0.42$ & [$-0.66$, $-0.18$] \\\\\nMistral-Large-2 & $-0.74$ & [$-1.10$, $-0.37$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Judge severity estimates ($d_j$) for all nine judges. Positive = stricter scoring. The severity range spans 1.34 logits (Claude Opus to Mistral), comparable to $\\sim$1 standard deviation of model ability.}\n\\label{tab:judge_severity}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Judge Count Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:judge_count}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:judge_count} reports ranking accuracy as a function of the number of judges $k$, obtained by subsampling from the full 9-judge panel (10 random subsets per $k$; $k = 9$ uses the full panel). G-MFRM outperforms both raw averaging and sequential estimation at all judge counts. Rank agreement increases monotonically with $k$ ($\\tau = 0.771 \\to 0.857$), and variance contracts as additional judges improve $d_j$ and $c_n$ estimation. At $k \\geq 4$, G-MFRM exceeds the full-panel baseline $\\tau = 0.786$, suggesting that four diverse judges suffice for practical deployment.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & Raw Avg & Sequential & G-MFRM & $\\Delta\\tau$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n2 & $.755 \\pm .088$ & $.764 \\pm .096$ & $.771 \\pm .081$ & $+.017$ \\\\\n4 & $.779 \\pm .041$ & $.786 \\pm .048$ & $.800 \\pm .045$ & $+.021$ \\\\\n6 & $.800 \\pm .030$ & $.807 \\pm .048$ & $.821 \\pm .051$ & $+.021$ \\\\\n9 & $.786$ & $.786$ & $.857$ & $+.071$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Kendall $\\tau$ with Chatbot Arena rankings as a function of judge count $k$. $\\Delta\\tau$ = G-MFRM $-$ Raw Average. Values for $k < 9$ are mean $\\pm$ std over 10 random judge subsets.}\n\\label{tab:judge_count}\n\\end{table}\n\nLeave-one-judge-out analysis reveals that three judges---Claude Opus 4.7 ($d_j = +0.60$), GPT-5.5 ($+0.53$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($-0.42$)---are individually necessary for the ranking improvement: removing any one of these reduces G-MFRM $\\tau$ to the baseline ($0.786$), while removing any of the other six judges preserves $\\tau = 0.857$. These three judges span the severity spectrum (the two strictest and the second-most lenient), suggesting that contamination$\\times$judge interaction identification requires sufficient severity diversity. This provides practical guidance: judge panels should include models with diverse scoring tendencies rather than homogeneous judges.\n\nG-MFRM requires $\\geq 4$ MCMC chains for reliable contamination estimation; reduced chain counts attenuate $c_n$ estimates toward the prior mean, compressing inter-model differences.\n\n\n\\section{Stage~2 Weight Robustness}\n\\label{app:weight_robustness}\n\nAll four weight configurations produce identical model rankings and near-perfect exposure score agreement (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.998$; Table~\\ref{tab:weight_robustness}), confirming that the default 75/25 split is not a sensitive hyperparameter choice.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Weight Config} & \\textbf{Exposure} $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n50 / 50 & 0.998 \\\\\n75 / 25 (default) & 1.000 \\\\\n90 / 10 & 0.999 \\\\\n100 / 0 & 0.998 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Stage~2 weight robustness. Weight Config denotes the percentage assigned to reference model anchoring vs.\\ cross-model agreement. All configurations produce identical model rankings. Exposure $\\rho$ is the Spearman correlation of per-item $e_i$ scores relative to the default 75/25 configuration.}\n\\label{tab:weight_robustness}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Power Analysis}\n\\label{app:power}\n\nThe observed effect size in the real-data evaluation is $\\Delta\\tau = 0.071$ (G-MFRM $\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ baseline $\\tau = 0.786$). We conduct a permutation-based power analysis to determine the sample sizes required to detect this effect at $\\alpha = 0.05$.\n\nWe simulate ranking comparisons under the alternative hypothesis $H_1\\!: \\Delta\\tau = 0.071$ by bootstrapping from the observed posterior distribution of G-MFRM ability estimates and recomputing $\\tau$ against ground truth at varying model counts $N$. For each $N$, we draw 10{,}000 permutation replicates and compute the rejection rate at $\\alpha = 0.05$ (two-sided).\n\nAt $N = 8$ (our evaluation), power is approximately 50\\%, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result ($\\Delta\\tau$ 95\\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$). Achieving 80\\% power requires $N \\approx 17$ models, and 95\\% power requires $N \\approx 25$. These estimates inform the scope of future evaluations: detecting ranking improvements of the magnitude observed here requires at minimum doubling the number of target models.\n\n\n\\section{Per-Model Estimates}\n\\label{app:raw_scores}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:raw_scores} reports G-MFRM posterior estimates for all eight target models. All seven baselines produce identical rankings (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), with ability scores differing by $< 0.03$ logits across methods; G-MFRM is the only method that alters the ranking, swapping gpt-4o-mini and Qwen2.5-72B at ranks 2--3.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & $\\hat{\\theta}$ [95\\% CI] & $\\hat{c}_n$ [95\\% CI] & \\textbf{Base} & \\textbf{G-MFRM} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-4o & $+2.00$ [$1.70$, $2.30$] & $0.88$ [$0.05$, $2.20$] & 1 & 1 \\\\\nGPT-4o-mini & $+1.40$ [$1.10$, $1.70$] & $0.93$ [$0.06$, $2.20$] & 3 & 2 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B & $+1.20$ [$0.85$, $1.50$] & $3.00$ [$1.40$, $4.60$] & 2 & 3 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.3-70B & $+0.54$ [$0.23$, $0.85$] & $2.50$ [$1.10$, $3.90$] & 4 & 4 \\\\\nQwen3-8B & $+0.51$ [$0.22$, $0.79$] & $1.20$ [$0.14$, $2.50$] & 5 & 5 \\\\\nGemma-2-9B & $-0.50$ [$-0.79$, $-0.21$] & $1.70$ [$0.36$, $2.90$] & 6 & 6 \\\\\nGPT-4-0613 & $-1.90$ [$-2.20$, $-1.60$] & $4.20$ [$3.10$, $5.30$] & 7 & 7 \\\\\nGPT-3.5-Turbo & $-3.30$ [$-3.60$, $-3.00$] & $5.60$ [$4.40$, $6.80$] & 8 & 8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{G-MFRM posterior estimates for ability ($\\hat{\\theta}$) and contamination susceptibility ($\\hat{c}_n$) with approximate 95\\% credible intervals (empirical coverage ${\\sim}$71\\%; see Limitations). Point estimates and rank orderings are the primary results. ``Base'' and ``G-MFRM'' columns show model rankings; the only rank change is the swap at positions 2--3.}\n\\label{tab:raw_scores}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Contamination Bias Absorption}\n\\label{app:bias_absorption}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:bias_absorption} compares per-model ability estimates from MFRM-only and G-MFRM. The difference $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{G-MFRM}}$ is perfectly rank-correlated with contamination susceptibility $c_n$ (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$), providing direct empirical evidence that MFRM-only absorbs the omitted contamination term into ability estimates.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & $\\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MFRM}}$ & $\\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{G-MFRM}}$ & $\\Delta\\theta$ & $\\hat{c}_n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-3.5-Turbo & $-2.487$ & $-3.274$ & $+0.786$ & $5.587$ \\\\\nGPT-4-0613 & $-1.446$ & $-1.877$ & $+0.431$ & $4.168$ \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B & $+1.213$ & $+1.178$ & $+0.035$ & $2.961$ \\\\\nLLaMA-3.3-70B & $+0.518$ & $+0.539$ & $-0.022$ & $2.478$ \\\\\nGemma-2-9B & $-0.661$ & $-0.500$ & $-0.161$ & $1.654$ \\\\\nQwen3-8B & $+0.208$ & $+0.513$ & $-0.305$ & $1.178$ \\\\\nGPT-4o-mini & $+1.064$ & $+1.432$ & $-0.368$ & $0.934$ \\\\\nGPT-4o & $+1.591$ & $+1.988$ & $-0.397$ & $0.880$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Contamination bias absorption in MFRM-only estimation. $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{G-MFRM}}$. Models with high contamination susceptibility ($c_n$) have inflated ability estimates under MFRM-only; models with low $c_n$ are relatively deflated. The perfect rank correlation (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$) between $\\Delta\\theta$ and $c_n$ empirically confirms the omitted variable bias predicted in Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}.}\n\\label{tab:bias_absorption}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Synthetic Calibration at Matched Scale}\n\\label{app:synthetic_calibration}\n\nWe generate synthetic data at matched scale ($N = 8$ models, $I = 176$ items, $J = 9$ judges) with known ground-truth parameters across 20 random seeds. Joint G-MFRM consistently outperforms sequential estimation: $\\theta$-RMSE is halved ($0.083$ vs.\\ $0.166$), and ranking agreement improves by 7.2 percentage points (mean $\\tau = 0.954$ vs.\\ $0.882$). Across 20 seeds, joint estimation improves or matches sequential in all cases (10 wins, 0 losses, 10 ties; Wilcoxon signed-rank $p < 0.002$). The synthetic effect size ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.072$) closely matches the observed real-data effect ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$). All 40 MCMC fits converge ($\\hat{R} = 1.0$, ESS $\\geq 1{,}678$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Sequential} & \\textbf{Joint G-MFRM} & \\textbf{Improv.} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\theta$ RMSE & $0.166 \\pm 0.050$ & $0.083 \\pm 0.024$ & $-50.3\\%$ \\\\\n$\\theta$ $\\tau$ & $0.882 \\pm 0.112$ & $0.954 \\pm 0.048$ & $+7.2$pp \\\\\n$c_n$ RMSE & $1.399 \\pm 0.358$ & $0.552 \\pm 0.328$ & $-60.5\\%$ \\\\\n$c_n$ Pearson & $0.850 \\pm 0.141$ & $0.866 \\pm 0.151$ & $+1.9$\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Synthetic calibration at $N = 8$ ($I = 176$, $J = 9$, 20 seeds). Joint G-MFRM halves $\\theta$-RMSE and reduces $c_n$-RMSE by 61\\% relative to sequential estimation.}\n\\label{tab:synthetic_calibration}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt $N = 20$ (20 seeds), the ranking improvement becomes individually significant: joint G-MFRM achieves $\\Delta\\tau = +0.052$ over sequential estimation (Wilcoxon $p = 1.81 \\times 10^{-4}$, 18 wins / 1 loss / 1 tie), directly validating the power analysis prediction that $N \\approx 17$ suffices for 80\\% power. The effect size is stable across scales ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.072$ at $N = 8$, $+0.052$ at $N = 20$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Sequential} & \\textbf{Joint G-MFRM} & \\textbf{Improv.} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\theta$ RMSE & $0.171 \\pm 0.049$ & $0.094 \\pm 0.019$ & $-45.2\\%$ \\\\\n$\\theta$ $\\tau$ & $0.891 \\pm 0.038$ & $0.943 \\pm 0.036$ & $+5.2$pp \\\\\n$c_n$ RMSE & $1.375 \\pm 0.278$ & $0.626 \\pm 0.263$ & $-54.5\\%$ \\\\\n$c_n$ Pearson & $0.817 \\pm 0.096$ & $0.842 \\pm 0.106$ & $+2.5\\%$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Synthetic calibration at $N = 20$ ($I = 176$, $J = 9$, 20 seeds). Ranking improvement is individually significant (Wilcoxon $p = 1.81 \\times 10^{-4}$), validating the power analysis prediction.}\n\\label{tab:synthetic_calibration_n20}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Prior Specification Robustness}\n\\label{app:prior_robustness}\n\nWe compare posterior estimates under two contamination priors: Horseshoe \\citep{piironen2017horseshoe} and hierarchical HalfNormal (Eq.~\\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Table~\\ref{tab:prior_robustness} reports $\\hat{c}_n$ and $\\hat{\\theta}$ posterior means for all eight target models under both specifications.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & $\\hat{c}_n$ (HS) & $\\hat{c}_n$ (HN) & $\\hat{\\theta}$ (HS) & $\\hat{\\theta}$ (HN) \\\\\n\\midrule\nGemma-2-9B & 1.61 & 1.65 & $-0.49$ & $-0.50$ \\\\\nGPT-3.5-Turbo & 5.69 & 5.57 & $-3.30$ & $-3.27$ \\\\\nGPT-4-0613 & 4.25 & 4.15 & $-1.89$ & $-1.88$ \\\\\nGPT-4o & 0.79 & 0.88 & $+2.01$ & $+1.99$ \\\\\nGPT-4o-mini & 0.84 & 0.92 & $+1.45$ & $+1.43$ \\\\\nLLaMA-3.3-70B & 2.50 & 2.46 & $+0.53$ & $+0.54$ \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B & 3.02 & 2.94 & $+1.17$ & $+1.18$ \\\\\nQwen3-8B & 1.11 & 1.15 & $+0.53$ & $+0.52$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Posterior estimates under Horseshoe (HS) and HalfNormal (HN) contamination priors. Both priors produce virtually identical estimates: Pearson $r = 1.000$ for $\\hat{\\theta}$ and $r = 0.9999$ for $\\hat{c}_n$.}\n\\label{tab:prior_robustness}\n\\end{table}\n\nPosterior estimates are virtually identical across the two prior specifications (Pearson $r = 1.000$ for $\\hat{\\theta}$, $r > 0.999$ for $\\hat{c}_n$), indicating that results are not sensitive to the choice of contamination prior.\n\n\n\\section{Item-Level Fit Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:item_diagnostics}\n\nWe report item-level infit and outfit MNSQ statistics for all 176 items. Items with MNSQ outside $[0.5, 1.5]$ are flagged as potentially misfitting.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Statistic} & \\textbf{Mean} & \\textbf{SD} & \\textbf{Median} & \\textbf{Min} & \\textbf{Max} \\\\\n\\midrule\nInfit MNSQ & 0.78 & 0.41 & 0.76 & 0.05 & 1.95 \\\\\nOutfit MNSQ & 0.73 & 0.57 & 0.58 & 0.04 & 3.17 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Item-level fit statistics across 176 items.}\n\\label{tab:item_fit_summary}\n\\end{table}\n\nOf the 176 items, 92 are flagged: 18 overfit (MNSQ $> 1.5$) and 74 underfit (MNSQ $< 0.5$). The majority of flagged items are underfit, reflecting sparse low-observation items where the model's predicted variance exceeds the observed variance---a consequence of limited data per item rather than a structural model deficiency. The 18 overfit items exhibit more erratic response patterns than the model predicts. Importantly, no model-level or judge-level facets are flagged (all MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$), confirming that the G-MFRM captures the systematic structure in model ability, judge severity, and contamination.\n\n\n\\section{CI Coverage Analysis}\n\\label{app:ci_coverage}\n\nWe assess empirical coverage of 95\\% posterior credible intervals across synthetic replications. At $N=8$ (49 seeds), ability parameter $\\theta$ achieves 90.1\\% coverage, approaching the nominal level, while contamination susceptibility $c_n$ achieves only 67.9\\%. At $N=50$ (50 seeds; exp-009), $c_n$ coverage improves to 71\\%. The coverage gap between $\\theta$ and $c_n$ reflects the multiplicative coupling $c_n \\cdot e_i$: contamination parameters enter the likelihood through an interaction term, making posterior inference more sensitive to exposure estimation noise and the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint at $c_n = 0$. These results support treating $c_n$ point estimates and rank orderings as reliable diagnostics, while noting that $c_n$ credible intervals are systematically too narrow.\n\n\\section{Use of AI Assistants}\n\\label{app:ai_assistants}\n\nWe used Claude (Anthropic) as an AI coding and writing assistant\nthroughout this work. Specifically:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Code:} drafting experiment scripts, data-processing\n    pipelines, and analysis utilities; debugging and refactoring.\n    \\item \\textbf{Writing:} drafting and revising paper sections,\n    polishing prose, and reformatting LaTeX.\n    \\item \\textbf{Literature:} summarizing related papers to inform\n    the related-work section.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{The G-MFRM Framework}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_error_propagation}\n\\caption{Error propagation from Stage~2 exposure noise to Stage~3 parameter estimates. Ability RMSE ($\\theta$, left) and contamination RMSE ($c_n$, right) as a function of exposure noise $\\sigma_e$. The dashed line marks $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}} \\approx 0.2$, below which degradation remains mild ($\\theta$-RMSE $+$5.9\\%, $c_n$-RMSE $+$7.9\\%). Error bars: $\\pm$1 std over 20 seeds.}\n\\label{fig:error_prop}\n\\end{figure}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:confounding} established that omitting contamination from the measurement model produces directional bias in ability estimates (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). We now introduce the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly estimates ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single measurement framework. The key design choices, staged estimation with externally fixed exposure and a hierarchical HalfNormal prior for the contamination term, are motivated by the identifiability analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:identifiability} and the error propagation bounds in \\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}.\n\n\\subsection{Model Specification}\n\\label{sec:model_spec}\n\nG-MFRM extends the standard MFRM \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} by adding a multiplicative contamination gating term to the linear predictor (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}):\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:gmfrm}\n\\log \\frac{P(Y_{nij} \\geq k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k)} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere all terms carry the definitions from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}. The gating structure $c_n \\cdot e_i$ separates the model-specific and item-specific components of contamination: $c_n \\geq 0$ captures how much model $n$ benefits from memorized benchmark content, while $e_i \\in [0,1]$ quantifies how much of item $i$ has leaked into training corpora. When $c_n = 0$ (a clean model) or $e_i = 0$ (a novel item), the gating term vanishes and Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm} reduces to the standard MFRM. This graceful degradation ensures that G-MFRM imposes no distortion on uncontaminated model-item pairs.\n\nWe enforce non-negativity and sparsity of $c_n$ through a hierarchical HalfNormal prior. A global shrinkage scale $\\sigma_c \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ controls the overall contamination level, and per-model raw effects $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ are scaled to obtain $c_n = c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c$. The HalfNormal support restricts $c_n \\geq 0$, and the hierarchical structure provides adaptive shrinkage: when the data support low overall contamination, $\\sigma_c$ contracts toward zero, pulling all $c_n$ values down. Models with genuine contamination retain large $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n$ relative to the group. The remaining priors are $\\theta_n \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $b_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $d_j \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$, and standard uniform on thresholds $\\tau_k$.\n\nG-MFRM extends the Doubly-Gated IRT model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt} from binary gating to continuous exposure and multi-rater designs, and contributes the first formal identifiability conditions for gated IRT parameters (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}, Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and error propagation bounds for staged estimation (\\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}).\n\n\\subsection{Staged Estimation}\n\\label{sec:staged}\n\nJoint estimation of all parameters in Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm}, including $e_i$, would require identifying the bilinear product $c_n \\cdot e_i$ from response data alone. This creates a fundamental non-identifiability (Proposition~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}). We resolve this through a three-stage procedure that fixes exposure externally before estimating the contamination parameters.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 1: Standard MFRM.} We fit the standard MFRM (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}) via MCMC (NUTS) to obtain initial estimates $(\\hat{\\theta}, \\hat{b}, \\hat{d})$. These serve as warm-start values for the sampler in Stage~3 and improve convergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 2: Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated from external evidence independent of the response data. The primary signal is \\emph{reference model anchoring} (75\\% weight): win-rate residuals against a fixed anchor (gpt-4-0314) flag items where target models outperform difficulty-adjusted expectations. The secondary signal is \\emph{cross-model agreement} (25\\%): uniformly high scores across diverse architectures suggest exposure rather than easiness. The composite is clipped to $[0, 1]$ and fixed in Stage~3. Estimation is non-circular: $e_i$ is item-level while $c_n$ is model-level, and ablation with 100\\% anchoring yields $\\rho = 0.998$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:weight_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed, we sample $(\\theta_n, b_i, d_j, c_n)$ jointly via the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) \\citep{betancourt2015}. The priors are as specified in \\S\\ref{sec:model_spec}. The hierarchical contamination prior decomposes as\n%\n\\begin{align}\n\\label{eq:contam_prior}\n\\sigma_c &\\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\quad c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\nonumber \\\\\nc_n &= c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c,\n\\end{align}\n%\nwhere $\\sigma_c$ is a shared global scale that governs the overall contamination magnitude. This non-centered parameterization improves sampler geometry by decoupling the global scale from the per-model effects.\n\nConvergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat} and bulk effective sample size (ESS), with thresholds $\\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS$_{\\mathrm{bulk}} > 100$ following standard practice \\citep{gelman2013bda}.\\footnote{Synthetic validation with $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$ yields satisfactory convergence across all parameters (exp-012). Full diagnostic tables are reported in Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}.}\n\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_cn_recovery}\n\\caption{Contamination susceptibility recovery ($c_n$: true vs.\\ estimated) from 50 Monte Carlo seeds. Empirical CI coverage is 71\\% (below nominal 95\\%), indicating moderate posterior overconfidence (see Limitations).}\n\\label{fig:cn_recovery}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAdding a multiplicative term to a measurement model raises the question of whether the new parameters can be separated from the existing ones. We establish that fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for identification. All proofs assume the standard location constraint $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[No New Indeterminacy]\n\\label{prop:no_new}\nWhen $e_i$ is fixed externally, the gating term $c_n \\cdot e_i$ introduces no additional location or scale indeterminacy beyond that already present in the standard MFRM.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Exposure Variation Necessary]\n\\label{prop:exposure_variation}\nIf $e_i = e$ for all $i$ (constant exposure), then $c_n$ and $\\theta_n$ are not separately identifiable.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{corollary}\n$\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ is a necessary condition for identifiability of $c_n$.\n\\end{corollary}\n\nProofs of Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new} and~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} are given in Appendix~\\ref{app:prop_proofs}.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Sufficient Conditions for Local Identifiability]\n\\label{thm:sufficient}\nThe G-MFRM is locally identifiable, up to the standard location constraint, if the following conditions hold: (i) a connected observation design (each item is observed by at least one model--judge pair), (ii) $J \\geq 2$ judges, (iii) for each model $n$, the set of observed items spans at least two distinct exposure values ($\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ over observed items), and (iv) standard normalization $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe proof (Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}) follows \\citet{rothenberg1971}, establishing Fisher information non-singularity via the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality on the $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix and a Schur complement for the remaining blocks.\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation}\n\\label{sec:error_prop}\n\nThe staged procedure treats the Stage~2 exposure estimates $\\hat{e}_i$ as fixed quantities in Stage~3. This is a generated regressors problem \\citep{pagan1984}: measurement error in $\\hat{e}_i$ propagates into the posterior of $(\\theta_n, c_n)$. The multiplicative structure $c_n \\cdot e_i$ makes this an errors-in-variables (EIV) problem \\citep{fuller1987}, with a predictable attenuation pattern.\n\nLet $\\hat{e}_i = e_i + \\epsilon_i$ with $\\epsilon_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, \\sigma^2)$ independent of all model parameters. The attenuation factor for the contamination coefficient is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:attenuation}\n\\lambda(\\sigma) = \\frac{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) + \\sigma^2},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nso attenuation reaches 50\\% when $\\sigma = \\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$. In the synthetic data-generating process ($\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) \\approx 0.04$), this corresponds to $\\sigma \\approx 0.2$.\\footnote{The attenuation formula assumes that $\\epsilon_i$ is independent of all model parameters, identically distributed, and Gaussian. In practice, exposure estimation errors may be correlated across items sharing the same source or heteroscedastic across difficulty levels. Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop} provides empirical validation that $\\hat{\\theta}$ estimates remain robust under such departures (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} shows the empirical degradation across noise levels (Figure~\\ref{fig:error_prop}). Below the critical threshold ($\\sigma \\leq 0.2$), degradation is mild: $\\theta$-RMSE increases by 5.9\\% (from $0.128 \\pm 0.018$ to $0.135 \\pm 0.018$) and $c_n$-RMSE by 7.9\\% (from $0.533 \\pm 0.187$ to $0.575 \\pm 0.198$). At $\\sigma = 0.1$, the increases are only 1.4\\% and 2.3\\% respectively. Beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes severe: $\\theta$-RMSE rises 36.9\\% and $c_n$-RMSE rises 57.9\\%. Contamination estimates $c_n$ are more sensitive than ability estimates $\\theta_n$ because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$ (multiplicative coupling), while $\\theta_n$ contributes additively and is anchored by all observations regardless of exposure.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\sigma$ & $\\theta$-RMSE & $\\Delta\\theta$\\% & $c_n$-RMSE & $\\Delta c_n$\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.0 & 0.128 $\\pm$ 0.018 & --- & 0.533 $\\pm$ 0.187 & --- \\\\\n0.1 & 0.130 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +1.4 & 0.545 $\\pm$ 0.192 & +2.3 \\\\\n0.2 & 0.135 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +5.9 & 0.575 $\\pm$ 0.198 & +7.9 \\\\\n0.5 & 0.175 $\\pm$ 0.027 & +36.9 & 0.842 $\\pm$ 0.281 & +57.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Error propagation robustness (exp-012). Ability RMSE degrades by less than 6\\% when exposure noise $\\sigma \\leq 0.2$, matching the theoretical prediction. Contamination estimates are more sensitive due to multiplicative coupling with $e_i$.}\n\\label{tab:error_prop}\n\\end{table}\n\nThese bounds make the staged approximation falsifiable: if the noise level of the Stage~2 exposure estimates can be bounded below $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}}$, the resulting ability estimates carry quantifiable error guarantees. For honest credible intervals that account for generated-regressor uncertainty, we apply the Murphy-Topel variance correction \\citep{murphy1985} to inflate the Stage~3 posterior variance by the estimated Stage~2 sampling variability (Appendix~\\ref{app:murphy_topel}).\n\n\\subsection{Fair Scores and Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:fair_scores}\n\nThe Stage~3 posterior mean of $\\theta_n$ provides a bias-and-contamination-corrected ``fair score'' for each model. The remaining parameters serve complementary diagnostic roles: per-model $c_n$ flags candidates for training data audit, per-judge $d_j$ informs judge calibration, and per-item $e_i$ identifies items for retirement or replacement.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KjZX1u8gHqiS4jjKDwGVyi`</summary>

```text
58	All seven baselines produce identical rankings ($\tau = 0.786$, $\rho = 0.905$; raw ability scores differ by $< 0.03$ logits, Table~\ref{tab:raw_scores}). G-MFRM achieves nominally higher rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$, $\rho = 0.952$), but bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ 95\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$ includes zero at $N = 8$, though $P(\Delta\tau \geq 0) = 0.927$. On synthetic data ($N = 50$), the improvement is significant ($\tau = 0.922$ vs.\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming the effect is real but below detection at this sample size.
59	
60	We evaluate G-MFRM along two orthogonal axes: predictive fit and ranking accuracy.
61	
62	\paragraph{Predictive fit.} Leave-one-out (LOO) cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM (expected log pointwise predictive density $\Delta\mathrm{ELPD}_{\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, standard error [SE] $= 9.4$, $|\Delta/\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable LOO estimates. The contamination gating term substantially improves held-out predictive fit.
63	
64	\paragraph{Ranking accuracy.} Ranking improvement ($\Delta\tau = +0.071$) is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\% CI crosses zero). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \approx 17$ is needed for 80\% power to detect this effect size (Appendix~\ref{app:power}). This prediction is empirically validated: at $N = 20$, synthetic $\Delta\tau$ reaches significance (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$, 18 wins / 1 loss; Appendix~\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}). Pooling 20 replications at $N = 8$ also yields significance ($p < 0.002$), confirming the method consistently improves rankings though any single $N = 8$ dataset has limited power.
65	
66	All parameters achieve $\hat{R} \leq 1.03$ with ESS $\geq 174$ and zero divergent transitions; model- and judge-level fit statistics fall within acceptable ranges (MNSQ $\in [0.63, 1.07]$; full diagnostics in Appendices~\ref{app:item_diagnostics} and~\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age (Figure~\ref{fig:cn_profile}a): the four oldest models show $\hat{c}_n > 1.0$ (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $5.60$; GPT-4-0613: $4.20$; Qwen2.5-72B: $3.00$; LLaMA-3.3-70B: $2.50$), while recent models show low contamination (GPT-4o: $0.88$; GPT-4o-mini: $0.93$). MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (Figure~\ref{fig:cn_profile}b; Table~\ref{tab:bias_absorption}). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges score more strictly, spanning 1.34 logits (Figure~\ref{fig:judge_severity}; Table~\ref{tab:judge_severity}). Results are robust to prior specification and judge panel size ($k \geq 4$ suffices; Appendices~\ref{app:prior_robustness} and~\ref{app:judge_count}).
67	
68	\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}
69	\label{sec:error_prop_results}
70	
71	Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} validates the theoretical prediction (\S\ref{sec:error_prop}): below $\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\%; beyond the threshold ($\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial. Ablation (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}, Appendix~\ref{app:ablation}) confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor ($2.0\times$ $\theta$-RMSE increase under staged estimation, $p < 0.001$) and continuous gating over binary ($c_n$-RMSE $-$40\%, $p < 0.001$).

      "content": "All seven baselines produce identical rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$, $\\rho = 0.905$; raw ability scores differ by $< 0.03$ logits, Table~\\ref{tab:raw_scores}). On synthetic data ($N = 50$), the improvement is significant ($\\tau = 0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming the effect is real but below detection at this sample size.\n\nWe evaluate G-MFRM along two orthogonal axes: predictive fit and ranking accuracy.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictive fit.} Leave-one-out (LOO) cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM (expected log pointwise predictive density $\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, standard error [SE] $= 9.4$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable LOO estimates. Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \\approx 17$ is needed for 80\\% power to detect this effect size (Appendix~\\ref{app:power}). This prediction is empirically validated: at $N = 20$, synthetic $\\Delta\\tau$ reaches significance (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$, 18 wins / 1 loss; Appendix~\\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}). Pooling 20 replications at $N = 8$ also yields significance ($p < 0.002$), confirming the method consistently improves rankings though any single $N = 8$ dataset has limited power.\n\nAll parameters achieve $\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ with ESS $\\geq 174$ and zero divergent transitions; model- and judge-level fit statistics fall within acceptable ranges (MNSQ $\\in [0.63, 1.07]$; full diagnostics in Appendices~\\ref{app:item_diagnostics} and~\\ref{app:ppc}). 89	The staged procedure treats the Stage~2 exposure estimates $\hat{e}_i$ as fixed quantities in Stage~3. This is a generated regressors problem \citep{pagan1984}: measurement error in $\hat{e}_i$ propagates into the posterior of $(\theta_n, c_n)$. The multiplicative structure $c_n \cdot e_i$ makes this an errors-in-variables (EIV) problem \citep{fuller1987}, with a predictable attenuation pattern.
90	
91	Let $\hat{e}_i = e_i + \epsilon_i$ with $\epsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)$ independent of all model parameters. The attenuation factor for the contamination coefficient is
92	%
93	\begin{equation}
94	\label{eq:attenuation}
95	\lambda(\sigma) = \frac{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}{\mathrm{Var}(e_i) + \sigma^2},
96	\end{equation}
97	%
98	so attenuation reaches 50\% when $\sigma = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$. In the synthetic data-generating process ($\mathrm{Var}(e_i) \approx 0.04$), this corresponds to $\sigma \approx 0.2$.\footnote{The attenuation formula assumes that $\epsilon_i$ is independent of all model parameters, identically distributed, and Gaussian. In practice, exposure estimation errors may be correlated across items sharing the same source or heteroscedastic across difficulty levels. Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} provides empirical validation that $\hat{\theta}$ estimates remain robust under such departures (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).}
99	
100	Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} and Figure~\ref{fig:error_prop} show empirical degradation: below $\sigma = 0.2$, $\theta$-RMSE increases by 5.9\% and $c_n$-RMSE by 7.9\%; beyond the threshold ($\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes severe. Contamination estimates are more sensitive because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$.
101	
102	\begin{table}[t]
103	\centering
104	\small
105	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
106	\toprule
107	$\sigma$ & $\theta$-RMSE & $\Delta\theta$\% & $c_n$-RMSE & $\Delta c_n$\% \\
108	\midrule
109	0.0 & 0.128 $\pm$ 0.018 & --- & 0.533 $\pm$ 0.187 & --- \\
110	0.1 & 0.130 $\pm$ 0.018 & +1.4 & 0.545 $\pm$ 0.192 & +2.3 \\
111	0.2 & 0.135 $\pm$ 0.018 & +5.9 & 0.575 $\pm$ 0.198 & +7.9 \\
112	0.5 & 0.175 $\pm$ 0.027 & +36.9 & 0.842 $\pm$ 0.281 & +57.9 \\
113	\bottomrule
114	\end{tabular}
115	\caption{Error propagation robustness (exp-012). Ability RMSE degrades by less than 6\% when exposure noise $\sigma \leq 0.2$, matching the theoretical prediction. Contamination estimates are more sensitive due to multiplicative coupling with $e_i$.}
116	\label{tab:error_prop}
117	\end{table}
118

117	\end{table}
118	
119	These bounds make the staged approximation falsifiable: if the noise level of the Stage~2 exposure estimates can be bounded below $\sigma_{\mathrm{crit}}$, the resulting ability estimates carry quantifiable error guarantees. For honest credible intervals that account for generated-regressor uncertainty, we apply the Murphy-Topel variance correction \citep{murphy1985} to inflate the Stage~3 posterior variance by the estimated Stage~2 sampling variability (Appendix~\ref{app:murphy_topel}).
120	
121	\subsection{Fair Scores and Diagnostics}
122	\label{sec:fair_scores}
123	
124	The Stage~3 posterior mean of $\theta_n$ provides a bias-and-contamination-corrected ``fair score'' for each model. The remaining parameters serve complementary diagnostic roles: per-model $c_n$ flags candidates for training data audit, per-judge $d_j$ informs judge calibration, and per-item $e_i$ identifies items for retirement or replacement.
