```json
    "oldString": "These bounds make the staged approximation falsifiable: if the noise level of the Stage~2 exposure estimates can be bounded below $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}}$, the resulting ability estimates carry quantifiable error guarantees. For honest credible intervals that account for generated-regressor uncertainty, we apply the Murphy-Topel variance correction \\citep{murphy1985} to inflate the Stage~3 posterior variance by the estimated Stage~2 sampling variability (Appendix~\\ref{app:murphy_topel}).",
    "newString": "For honest credible intervals, we apply the Murphy--Topel variance correction \\citep{murphy1985} to account for generated-regressor uncertainty (Appendix~\\ref{app:murphy_topel}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{The G-MFRM Framework}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_error_propagation}\n\\caption{Error propagation from Stage~2 exposure noise to Stage~3 parameter estimates. Ability RMSE ($\\theta$, left) and contamination RMSE ($c_n$, right) as a function of exposure noise $\\sigma_e$. The dashed line marks $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}} \\approx 0.2$, below which degradation remains mild ($\\theta$-RMSE $+$5.9\\%, $c_n$-RMSE $+$7.9\\%). Error bars: $\\pm$1 std over 20 seeds.}\n\\label{fig:error_prop}\n\\end{figure}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:confounding} established that omitting contamination from the measurement model produces directional bias in ability estimates (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). We now introduce the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly estimates ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single measurement framework. The key design choices, staged estimation with externally fixed exposure and a hierarchical HalfNormal prior for the contamination term, are motivated by the identifiability analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:identifiability} and the error propagation bounds in \\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}.\n\n\\subsection{Model Specification}\n\\label{sec:model_spec}\n\nG-MFRM extends the standard MFRM \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} by adding a multiplicative contamination gating term to the linear predictor (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}):\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:gmfrm}\n\\log \\frac{P(Y_{nij} \\geq k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k)} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere all terms carry the definitions from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}. The gating structure $c_n \\cdot e_i$ separates the model-specific and item-specific components of contamination: $c_n \\geq 0$ captures how much model $n$ benefits from memorized benchmark content, while $e_i \\in [0,1]$ quantifies how much of item $i$ has leaked into training corpora. When $c_n = 0$ (a clean model) or $e_i = 0$ (a novel item), the gating term vanishes and Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm} reduces to the standard MFRM. This graceful degradation ensures that G-MFRM imposes no distortion on uncontaminated model-item pairs.\n\nWe enforce non-negativity and sparsity of $c_n$ through a hierarchical HalfNormal prior. A global shrinkage scale $\\sigma_c \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ controls the overall contamination level, and per-model raw effects $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ are scaled to obtain $c_n = c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c$. The HalfNormal support restricts $c_n \\geq 0$, and the hierarchical structure provides adaptive shrinkage: when the data support low overall contamination, $\\sigma_c$ contracts toward zero, pulling all $c_n$ values down. Models with genuine contamination retain large $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n$ relative to the group. The remaining priors are $\\theta_n \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $b_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $d_j \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$, and standard uniform on thresholds $\\tau_k$.\n\nG-MFRM extends the Doubly-Gated IRT model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt} from binary gating to continuous exposure and multi-rater designs, and contributes the first formal identifiability conditions for gated IRT parameters (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}, Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and error propagation bounds for staged estimation (\\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}).\n\n\\subsection{Staged Estimation}\n\\label{sec:staged}\n\nJoint estimation of all parameters in Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm}, including $e_i$, would require identifying the bilinear product $c_n \\cdot e_i$ from response data alone. This creates a fundamental non-identifiability (Proposition~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}). We resolve this through a three-stage procedure that fixes exposure externally before estimating the contamination parameters.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 1: Standard MFRM.} We fit the standard MFRM (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}) via MCMC (NUTS) to obtain initial estimates $(\\hat{\\theta}, \\hat{b}, \\hat{d})$. These serve as warm-start values for the sampler in Stage~3 and improve convergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 2: Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated from external evidence independent of the response data. The primary signal is \\emph{reference model anchoring} (75\\% weight): win-rate residuals against a fixed anchor (gpt-4-0314) flag items where target models outperform difficulty-adjusted expectations. The secondary signal is \\emph{cross-model agreement} (25\\%): uniformly high scores across diverse architectures suggest exposure rather than easiness. The composite is clipped to $[0, 1]$ and fixed in Stage~3. Estimation is non-circular: $e_i$ is item-level while $c_n$ is model-level, and ablation with 100\\% anchoring yields $\\rho = 0.998$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:weight_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed, we sample $(\\theta_n, b_i, d_j, c_n)$ jointly via the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) \\citep{betancourt2015}. The priors are as specified in \\S\\ref{sec:model_spec}. The hierarchical contamination prior decomposes as\n%\n\\begin{align}\n\\label{eq:contam_prior}\n\\sigma_c &\\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\quad c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\nonumber \\\\\nc_n &= c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c,\n\\end{align}\n%\nwhere $\\sigma_c$ is a shared global scale that governs the overall contamination magnitude. This non-centered parameterization improves sampler geometry by decoupling the global scale from the per-model effects.\n\nConvergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat} and bulk effective sample size (ESS), with thresholds $\\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS$_{\\mathrm{bulk}} > 100$ following standard practice \\citep{gelman2013bda}.\\footnote{Synthetic validation with $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$ yields satisfactory convergence across all parameters (exp-012). Full diagnostic tables are reported in Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}.}\n\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_cn_recovery}\n\\caption{Contamination susceptibility recovery ($c_n$: true vs.\\ estimated) from 50 Monte Carlo seeds. Empirical CI coverage is 71\\% (below nominal 95\\%), indicating moderate posterior overconfidence (see Limitations).}\n\\label{fig:cn_recovery}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAdding a multiplicative term to a measurement model raises the question of whether the new parameters can be separated from the existing ones. We establish that fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for identification. All proofs assume the standard location constraint $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[No New Indeterminacy]\n\\label{prop:no_new}\nWhen $e_i$ is fixed externally, the gating term $c_n \\cdot e_i$ introduces no additional location or scale indeterminacy beyond that already present in the standard MFRM.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Exposure Variation Necessary]\n\\label{prop:exposure_variation}\nIf $e_i = e$ for all $i$ (constant exposure), then $c_n$ and $\\theta_n$ are not separately identifiable.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{corollary}\n$\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ is a necessary condition for identifiability of $c_n$.\n\\end{corollary}\n\nProofs of Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new} and~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} are given in Appendix~\\ref{app:prop_proofs}.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Sufficient Conditions for Local Identifiability]\n\\label{thm:sufficient}\nThe G-MFRM is locally identifiable, up to the standard location constraint, if the following conditions hold: (i) a connected observation design (each item is observed by at least one model--judge pair), (ii) $J \\geq 2$ judges, (iii) for each model $n$, the set of observed items spans at least two distinct exposure values ($\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ over observed items), and (iv) standard normalization $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe proof (Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}) follows \\citet{rothenberg1971}, establishing Fisher information non-singularity via the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality on the $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix and a Schur complement for the remaining blocks.\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation}\n\\label{sec:error_prop}\n\nThe staged procedure treats the Stage~2 exposure estimates $\\hat{e}_i$ as fixed quantities in Stage~3. This is a generated regressors problem \\citep{pagan1984}: measurement error in $\\hat{e}_i$ propagates into the posterior of $(\\theta_n, c_n)$. The multiplicative structure $c_n \\cdot e_i$ makes this an errors-in-variables (EIV) problem \\citep{fuller1987}, with a predictable attenuation pattern.\n\nLet $\\hat{e}_i = e_i + \\epsilon_i$ with $\\epsilon_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, \\sigma^2)$ independent of all model parameters. The attenuation factor for the contamination coefficient is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:attenuation}\n\\lambda(\\sigma) = \\frac{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) + \\sigma^2},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nso attenuation reaches 50\\% when $\\sigma = \\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$. In the synthetic data-generating process ($\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) \\approx 0.04$), this corresponds to $\\sigma \\approx 0.2$.\\footnote{The attenuation formula assumes that $\\epsilon_i$ is independent of all model parameters, identically distributed, and Gaussian. In practice, exposure estimation errors may be correlated across items sharing the same source or heteroscedastic across difficulty levels. Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop} provides empirical validation that $\\hat{\\theta}$ estimates remain robust under such departures (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_prop} show empirical degradation: below $\\sigma = 0.2$, $\\theta$-RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and $c_n$-RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes severe. Contamination estimates are more sensitive because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\sigma$ & $\\theta$-RMSE & $\\Delta\\theta$\\% & $c_n$-RMSE & $\\Delta c_n$\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.0 & 0.128 $\\pm$ 0.018 & --- & 0.533 $\\pm$ 0.187 & --- \\\\\n0.1 & 0.130 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +1.4 & 0.545 $\\pm$ 0.192 & +2.3 \\\\\n0.2 & 0.135 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +5.9 & 0.575 $\\pm$ 0.198 & +7.9 \\\\\n0.5 & 0.175 $\\pm$ 0.027 & +36.9 & 0.842 $\\pm$ 0.281 & +57.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Error propagation robustness (exp-012). Ability RMSE degrades by less than 6\\% when exposure noise $\\sigma \\leq 0.2$, matching the theoretical prediction. Contamination estimates are more sensitive due to multiplicative coupling with $e_i$.}\n\\label{tab:error_prop}\n\\end{table}\n\nThese bounds make the staged approximation falsifiable: if the noise level of the Stage~2 exposure estimates can be bounded below $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}}$, the resulting ability estimates carry quantifiable error guarantees. For honest credible intervals that account for generated-regressor uncertainty, we apply the Murphy-Topel variance correction \\citep{murphy1985} to inflate the Stage~3 posterior variance by the estimated Stage~2 sampling variability (Appendix~\\ref{app:murphy_topel}).\n\n\\subsection{Fair Scores and Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:fair_scores}\n\nThe Stage~3 posterior mean of $\\theta_n$ provides a bias-and-contamination-corrected ``fair score'' for each model. The remaining parameters serve complementary diagnostic roles: per-model $c_n$ flags candidates for training data audit, per-judge $d_j$ informs judge calibration, and per-item $e_i$ identifies items for retirement or replacement.",
```

```text
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{forest_dj}
7	\caption{Judge severity estimates ($d_j$) for all nine judges with 94\% credible intervals. Positive values indicate stricter scoring. Proprietary judges (Claude Opus, GPT-5.5) cluster at the strict end; open-source judges (Mistral, LLaMA) are more lenient. The severity range spans 1.34 logits.}
8	\label{fig:judge_severity}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	\subsection{Setup}
12	\label{sec:setup}
13	
14	\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 316 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. After filtering for coverage, the evaluation set comprises 176 items in a connected incomplete design (XX{,}XXX observations; XX.X\% fill rate of the full $N \times I \times J$ tensor). Seventeen target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated: the original 8 models (GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, Qwen2.5-72B, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen3-8B, Gemma-2-9B, GPT-4-0613, GPT-3.5-Turbo) plus 9 additional models (Claude-3.5-Sonnet, GPT-4-Turbo, LLaMA-3.1-70B, Gemma-2-27B, Claude-3-Opus, LLaMA-3-70B, Mixtral-8x22B, Claude-3-Haiku, LLaMA-3-8B). Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \citep{zheng2023mtbench}.
15	
16	\paragraph{Judges.} Nine LLM judges score model responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.7, Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Mistral-Large-2, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Seven judges cover all 17 target models; two judges (GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7) cover only the original 8 models, yielding an asymmetric connected design---Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only connectivity, not full crossing. Position swapping controls for position bias and doubles the effective observation count. Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs systematic self-evaluation bias, and excluding self-judging observations leaves rankings unchanged ($\tau = 0.857$; Appendix~\ref{app:self_eval}).
17	
18	\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated via a two-source pipeline (\S\ref{sec:staged}): reference model anchoring with gpt-4-0314 as anchor (75\% weight) and cross-model agreement (25\% weight). The pipeline is robust to weight configuration (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.998$ across all configurations tested).
19	
20	\paragraph{MCMC.} We use NumPyro's NUTS sampler with 4{,}000 warmup and 4{,}000 sampling iterations across 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). The contamination prior follows the hierarchical HalfNormal specification (Eq.~\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\hat{R}$ \citep{vehtari2021rhat} with conventional $\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS $> 100$ thresholds \citep{gelman2013bda}.
21	
22	\subsection{Baselines and Main Results}
23	\label{sec:main_results}
24	
25	We compare G-MFRM against seven methods that address judge bias or contamination separately: (1) Raw Average, (2) MFRM-only (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}), (3) Sequential pipeline (MFRM then contamination adjustment), (4) CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026}, (5) CyclicJudge+discard (exposed items removed), (6) DCR Factor \citep{dcr2025}, and (7) Preference Leakage calibration \citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\tau$ and Spearman $\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.
26	
27	\begin{table}[t]
28	\centering
29	\small
30	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
31	\toprule
32	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{8 models}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{17 models}} \\
33	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5}
34	\textbf{Method} & $\tau$ & $\rho$ & $\tau$ & $\rho$ \\
35	\midrule
36	Raw Average & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.838 & 0.949 \\
37	MFRM-only & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\
38	Sequential & 0.786 & 0.905 & TBD & TBD \\
39	CyclicJudge & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.838 & 0.949 \\
40	CyclicJudge+discard & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.824 & 0.934 \\
41	DCR Factor & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.824 & 0.946 \\
42	Pref.\ Leakage & 0.786 & 0.905 & 0.838 & 0.949 \\
43	\midrule
44	G-MFRM (ours) & \textbf{0.857} & \textbf{0.952} & \textbf{TBD} & \textbf{TBD} \\
45	\bottomrule
46	\end{tabular}
47	\caption{Correlation with Chatbot Arena human rankings on Arena-Hard (9 judges). All seven baselines produce identical rankings at $N = 8$ ($\tau = 0.786$). G-MFRM yields nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($+$7.1pp; bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ CI includes zero). Right columns report the 17-model evaluation (TBD).}
48	\label{tab:main_results}
49	\end{table}
50	
51	\begin{figure*}[t]
52	\centering
53	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig6_cn_profile}
54	\caption{Per-model contamination diagnostics from G-MFRM. (a)~Posterior estimates of contamination susceptibility $\hat{c}_n$ with 95\% credible intervals, ordered by magnitude. Older models (GPT-3.5-Turbo, GPT-4-0613) show 4--6$\times$ higher susceptibility than recent models (GPT-4o). (b)~Ability bias absorbed by MFRM-only estimation: $\Delta\theta = \hat{\theta}_{\mathrm{MFRM}} - \hat{\theta}_{\mathrm{G\text{-}MFRM}}$. The perfect rank correlation (Spearman $\rho = 1.0$) confirms that contamination inflates MFRM ability estimates for high-$c_n$ models and deflates them for low-$c_n$ models, as predicted by the OVB analysis (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}).}
55	\label{fig:cn_profile}
56	\end{figure*}
57	
58	All seven baselines produce identical rankings ($\tau = 0.786$, $\rho = 0.905$; raw ability scores differ by $< 0.03$ logits, Table~\ref{tab:raw_scores}). G-MFRM achieves nominally higher rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$, $\rho = 0.952$), but bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ 95\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$ includes zero at $N = 8$, though $P(\Delta\tau \geq 0) = 0.927$. On synthetic data ($N = 50$), the improvement is significant ($\tau = 0.922$ vs.\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming the effect is real but below detection at this sample size.
59	
60	\paragraph{Predictive fit.} LOO cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM ($\Delta\mathrm{ELPD}_{\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, SE $= 9.4$, $|\Delta/\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 and all Pareto $\hat{k} < 0.7$.
61	
62	\paragraph{Ranking accuracy.} The $\Delta\tau = +0.071$ improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\% CI crosses zero; post-hoc power ${\sim}$50\%). Power analysis indicates $N \approx 17$ for 80\% power (Appendix~\ref{app:power}), validated at $N = 20$ where the improvement reaches significance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}).
63	
64	All parameters achieve $\hat{R} \leq 1.03$ with ESS $\geq 174$ and zero divergent transitions; model- and judge-level fit statistics fall within acceptable ranges (MNSQ $\in [0.63, 1.07]$; full diagnostics in Appendices~\ref{app:item_diagnostics} and~\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age (Figure~\ref{fig:cn_profile}a): the four oldest models show $\hat{c}_n > 1.0$ (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $5.60$; GPT-4-0613: $4.20$; Qwen2.5-72B: $3.00$; LLaMA-3.3-70B: $2.50$), while recent models show low contamination (GPT-4o: $0.88$; GPT-4o-mini: $0.93$). MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (Figure~\ref{fig:cn_profile}b; Table~\ref{tab:bias_absorption}). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges score more strictly, spanning 1.34 logits (Figure~\ref{fig:judge_severity}; Table~\ref{tab:judge_severity}). Results are robust to prior specification and judge panel size ($k \geq 4$ suffices; Appendices~\ref{app:prior_robustness} and~\ref{app:judge_count}).
65	
66	\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}
67	\label{sec:error_prop_results}
68	
69	Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} validates the theoretical prediction (\S\ref{sec:error_prop}): below $\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\%; beyond the threshold ($\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial. Ablation (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}, Appendix~\ref{app:ablation}) confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor ($2.0\times$ $\theta$-RMSE increase under staged estimation, $p < 0.001$) and continuous gating over binary ($c_n$-RMSE $-$40\%, $p < 0.001$).
70	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}
5	LLM-as-a-judge evaluations exhibit position bias \citep{wang2024positionbias}, self-preference \citep{panickssery2024selffavor}, and other cognitive biases \citep{koo2024cobbler, ye2024justice, bavaresco2025judgebench}; see also \citet{li2024splitmerge} and \citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty}. On the correction side, CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias via round-robin assignment, and UDA \citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\%. All of these methods model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination effects into ability estimates (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Benchmark Contamination Detection and Avoidance.}
8	Detection methods include performance-based tests \citep{constat2024}, unified contamination metrics \citep{dcr2025}, token-level log-probability probes \citep{logprober2024, mink2024}, and guided prompting \citep{golchin2024timetravel, oren2024proving}; see also \citet{deng2024contamination, sainz2023contamination}. \citet{preference_leakage2026} identify judge-side preference leakage, complementary to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus. Avoidance strategies include dynamic benchmarks \citep{livebench2025}, private evaluation \citep{truce2024}, and subset selection \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks}. G-MFRM instead corrects for residual contamination while simultaneously modeling judge effects.
9	
10	\paragraph{Item Response Theory (IRT) and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}
11	IRT has been applied to NLP leaderboards for ranking and benchmark analysis \citep{lalor2019irt, rodriguez2021irt, vania2021irt}. The Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) \citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} extends IRT to multi-rater settings; \citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply it to LLM judge panels, and \citet{uto2020generalized} and \citet{choi2026irt_judge} propose Bayesian variants; see also \citet{zhou2025psnirt}. The Doubly-Gated IRT (DG-IRT) model of \citet{shu2013dgirt} is the closest antecedent, introducing binary gating for item preknowledge; G-MFRM extends this to continuous exposure, multi-rater designs, and formal identifiability analysis (Section~\ref{sec:model_spec}).
12	
```

All of these methods model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination effects into ability estimates (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Contamination Detection and Avoidance.}\nDetection methods include performance-based tests \\citep{constat2024}, unified contamination metrics \\citep{dcr2025}, token-level log-probability probes \\citep{logprober2024, mink2024}, and guided prompting \\citep{golchin2024timetravel, oren2024proving}; see also \\citet{deng2024contamination, sainz2023contamination}. \\citet{preference_leakage2026} identify judge-side preference leakage, complementary to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus. Avoidance strategies include dynamic benchmarks \\citep{livebench2025}, private evaluation \\citep{truce2024}, and subset selection \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks}. G-MFRM instead corrects for residual contamination while simultaneously modeling judge effects.\n\n\\paragraph{Item Response Theory (IRT) and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}\nIRT has been applied to NLP leaderboards for ranking and benchmark analysis \\citep{lalor2019irt, rodriguez2021irt, vania2021irt}. The Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} extends IRT to multi-rater settings; \\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply it to LLM judge panels, and \\citet{uto2020generalized} and \\citet{choi2026irt_judge} propose Bayesian variants; see also \\citet{zhou2025psnirt}. The Doubly-Gated IRT (DG-IRT) model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt} is the closest antecedent, introducing binary gating for item preknowledge; G-MFRM extends this to continuous exposure, multi-rater designs, and formal identifiability analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:model_spec}).\n",
```json
      "numLines": 12,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 12
    }
  }
}
```

```text
1	\section{Confounding in LLM Evaluation}
2	\label{sec:confounding}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig2_ovb_bias}
7	\caption{Sequential estimation bias ($\hat{\theta}_{\mathrm{seq}} - \theta_{\mathrm{true}}$) versus true contamination susceptibility $c_n$. Pearson $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$) confirms systematic overestimation of contaminated models. Synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds).}
8	\label{fig:ovb_bias}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	The Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) extends classical Rasch measurement to settings with multiple rater facets \citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm}. Originating in educational measurement, the model treats rater variation as a nuisance facet to be estimated and removed rather than a source of noise to be averaged over. In the LLM evaluation context, where $J$ judges score $N$ models across $I$ items, the MFRM specifies
12	%
13	\begin{equation}
14	\label{eq:mfrm}
15	\log \frac{P(Y_{nij} \geq k \mid \theta_n, b_i, d_j, \tau_k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k \mid \theta_n, b_i, d_j, \tau_k)} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k,
16	\end{equation}
17	%
18	where $Y_{nij}$ is the ordinal rating by judge $j$ for model $n$ on item $i$, $\theta_n$ is the ability of model $n$ ($n = 1, \ldots, N$), $b_i$ is the difficulty of item $i$ ($i = 1, \ldots, I$), $d_j$ is the severity of judge $j$ ($j = 1, \ldots, J$), and $\tau_k$ is the threshold for category $k$. All facets reside on a common logit scale, so model ability is directly comparable after adjustment for item difficulty and judge severity. \citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} applied this framework to compare ten LLM raters with human experts, showing that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects in multi-judge panels.
19	
20	However, standard MFRM assumes that all systematic sources of score variation are captured by the modeled facets. This assumption breaks down when some models have been exposed to benchmark items during training \citep{dcr2025}. Such contamination inflates scores on exposed items through memorization rather than genuine ability. We represent this effect as an interaction $c_n \cdot e_i$, where $c_n \geq 0$ is the contamination susceptibility of model $n$ and $e_i \in [0, 1]$ is the exposure level of item $i$. The complete data-generating process becomes
21	%
22	\begin{equation}
23	\label{eq:full}
24	\eta_{nijk} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i.
25	\end{equation}
26	
27	\begin{figure}[t]
28	\centering
29	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_bias_vs_rho}
30	\caption{Sequential estimator bias remains approximately constant across confounding levels ($\rho$), confirming omission-driven rather than confounding-strength-dependent bias. Joint G-MFRM estimation reduces bias by approximately 70\% at all $\rho$ levels. Synthetic data: $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 50 seeds.}
31	\label{fig:bias_vs_rho}
32	\end{figure}
33	
34	When a practitioner fits the standard MFRM (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}) to data generated by Eq.~\ref{eq:full}, the contamination term is absent from the estimated model. Its influence does not vanish but is absorbed into the estimated parameters $\tilde{\theta}_n$, $\tilde{b}_i$, and $\tilde{d}_j$, which distorts all downstream inferences.
35	
36	The consequence of this omission can be stated precisely.
37	
38	\begin{observation}[Omitted Variable Bias in MFRM]
39	\label{obs:ovb}
40	Let the full model be $\eta_{nijk} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i$ and the short model omit the contamination term: $\tilde{\eta}_{nijk} = \tilde{\theta}_n - \tilde{b}_i - \tilde{d}_j - \tau_k$. Under a linear approximation to the ordinal logit link \citep{wooldridge2010, angrist2009mostly},\footnote{The OVB formula is exact for linear models. We apply it to the MFRM linear predictor $\eta$, which is an approximation. Synthetic validation confirms the approximation quality: the Pearson correlation between the predicted bias direction and empirical bias reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), indicating that the linear result accurately characterizes bias direction and relative magnitude.}
41	\begin{equation}
42	\label{eq:ovb}
43	\mathrm{plim}(\tilde{\theta}_n - \theta_n) = \gamma_n \cdot c_n,
44	\end{equation}
45	where $\gamma_n$ depends on the projection of the exposure vector $(e_i)$ onto the design matrix of the short model (explicit form in Appendix~\ref{app:gamma}). The bias is non-zero whenever $\mathrm{Cov}(d_j,\, c_n \cdot e_i) \neq 0$. This captures the confounding component; in the nonlinear MFRM, a specification bias from omitting $c_n \cdot e_i$ persists even at zero covariance (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
46	\end{observation}
47	
48	G-MFRM's Stage~3 inference (Eq.~\ref{eq:gmfrm}) uses the full nonlinear ordinal logistic likelihood; the linear approximation serves only as directional motivation. Monte Carlo simulation confirms that bias magnitude is approximately constant across confounding levels $\rho$ (Figure~\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho}), indicating that omission of $c_n \cdot e_i$---not its correlation with $d_j$---is the primary driver. Neither sequential ordering resolves this: bias-first approaches \citep{cyclicjudge2026} absorb contamination into $\hat{\theta}$, while contamination-first approaches \citep{dcr2025} misattribute judge severity as contamination. Synthetic validation ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds) confirms directional bias ($r = 0.86$, $p < 0.001$; Figure~\ref{fig:ovb_bias}).
```

```json
{
```
```json
  "command": "python3 -c \"\nimport fitz\ndoc = fitz.open('main.pdf')\nprint(f'Total pages: {len(doc)}')\nfor page_num in range(min(12, len(doc))):\n    page = doc[page_num]\n    text = page.get_text()\n    for kw in ['Introduction', 'Related Work', 'Confounding', 'The G-MFRM', 'Experiments', 'Conclusion', 'Limitations', 'References']:\n        lines = text.split('\\n')\n        for line in lines:\n            s = line.strip()\n            # Match section headers\n            if s == kw or s.endswith(kw):\n                if len(s) < 40:\n                    idx = text.index(s)\n                    pct = idx / len(text) * 100\n                    print(f'  Page {page_num+1} ({pct:.0f}%): {s}')\n\" 2>/dev/null"
}
```
